(1/\eta)^{(\alpha-1)l_{i}^{*}}
.
Moreover, whenever it happens, it is almost always because
X^{\eta}_{n}
is shaken by
exactly
l^{*}_{i}
large gradient noises of size
\mathcal{O}(1/\eta)
, which translates to
l^{*}_{i}
jumps in
X_{\lceil n/\eta\rceil}^{\eta}
’s path, while the rest of its path closely resemble the deterministic gradient flow.
Moreover, conditional on the event that
X_{n}^{\eta}
fails to escape from
\Omega_{i}
within this period, the endpoint of the path is most likely to be close to the local minima, i.e.,
X_{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}^{\eta}\approx m_{i}
.
This suggests that over the next time period
H_{2}\triangleq\{\lceil t/\eta\rceil+1,\lceil t/\eta\rceil+2,\ldots,2\lceil t/\eta\rceil\}
of length
\lceil t/\eta\rceil
,
X_{n}^{\eta}
will behave similarly to its behavior over the first period
H_{1}
.
The same argument applies to the subsequent periods
H_{3},H_{4},\ldots
as well.
Therefore, over each time period of length
\lceil t/\eta\rceil
, there is
(1/\eta)^{(\alpha-1)l_{i}^{*}}
probability of exit.
In view of this, the exit time should be of order
(1/\eta)^{1+(\alpha-1)l^{*}_{i}}
and resemble an exponential distribution when scaled properly.

•

Part (i) of Theorem
1
builds on this intuition and rigorously prove that the first exit time is indeed roughly of order
1/\lambda_{i}(\eta)\approx(1/\eta)^{1+(\alpha-1)l^{*}_{i}}
and resembles an exponential distribution.

•

Given this, one would expect that
X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{i}(\eta)}
, the location of SGD right at the time of exit, will hardly ever be farther than
l^{*}_{i}b
away from
m_{i}
: the length of each update is clipped by
b
, and there will most likely be only
l^{*}_{i}
large SGD steps during this successful attempt.
Indeed, from the definition of
q_{i,j}
’s above, one can see that
q_{i,j}>0
if and only if
\inf_{y\in\Omega_{j}}|y-m_{i}|<l^{*}_{i}b
.

Summarizing the three bullet points here, we see that the minimum number of jumps
l^{*}_{i}
dictates
how
heavy-tailed SGD escapes an attraction field,
where
the SGD lands on upon its exit, and
when
the exit occurs.

Now we are ready to start proving Theorem
1
. First, note that Assumption 1 implies the following:

•

There exist
c_{0}>0,\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that for any
x\in\{m_{1},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n_{\text{min}}-1},m_{n_{\text{min}}}\},|y-x|<\epsilon_{0}
,

\displaystyle|f^{\prime}(y)|>c_{0}|y-x|,

(G.8)

and for any
y\in[-L,L]
such that
|y-x|\geq\epsilon_{0}
for all
x\in\{m_{1},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n_{\text{min}}-1},m_{n_{\text{min}}}\}
, we have

\displaystyle|f^{\prime}(y)|>c_{0};

(G.9)

•

There exist constants
L\in(0,\infty),M\in(0,\infty)
such that
|m_{0}|<L,|m_{n_{\text{min}}}|<L
, and (for any
x\in[-L,L]
)

\displaystyle|f^{\prime}(x)|\leq M,\ |f^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq M.

(G.10)

Recall that for
c>0
, the truncation operator was defined as

\displaystyle\varphi_{c}(w)\triangleq\varphi(w,c)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}(w\wedge c)\vee(-c),\ \ \ \forall w\in\mathbb{R},

(G.11)

and the SGD iterates were defined as

\displaystyle X^{\eta}_{n}=\varphi_{L}\Big(X^{\eta}_{n}-\varphi_{b}\big(\eta(f^{\prime}(X^{\eta}_{n})-Z_{n+1})\big)\Big).

(G.12)

Here
\eta>0
is the learning rate (step length) and
b>0
is the gradient clipping threshold. Also, recall that for any
k\in[n_{\text{min}}]
, we let

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}(\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n\geq 0:\ X^{\eta}_{n}\notin\Omega_{k}\}

to be the time that
X^{\eta}_{n}
exists from the
k-
th attraction field
\Omega_{j}
. Meanwhile, given the gradient clipping threshold
b
, recall that

\displaystyle r_{i}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{m_{i}-s_{i-1},s_{i}-m_{i}\},

(G.13)

\displaystyle l^{*}_{i}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\lceil r_{i}/b\rceil.

(G.14)

Intuitively speaking,
l^{*}_{i}
tells us the minimum number of jumps with size no larger than
b
required in order to escape the attraction field if we start from the local minimum of this attraction field
\Omega_{i}
. Lastly, recall the definition of
H(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(|Z_{1}|>\cdot)
and

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}H(1/\eta)\Big(\frac{H(1/\eta)}{\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}_{i}-1}.

The proof of Theorem
1
hinges on the following two lemmas that characterize the behavior of
X^{\eta}_{n}
in two different phases respectively. Let
k\in[n_{\text{min}}]
and
x\in\Omega_{k}
.
We consider the SGD iterates initialized at
X^{\eta}_{0}=x
. In the first phase, the SGD iterates return to
[m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon]
, a small neighborhood of the local minimizer in attraction field
\Omega_{k}
; in other words, it ends at

\displaystyle T_{\text{return}}^{(k)}(\eta,\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n\geq 0:\ X^{\eta}_{n}\in[m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon]\}.

(G.15)

During this phase, we show that for all learning rate
\eta
that is sufficiently small, it is almost always the case that
X^{\eta}_{n}
would quickly return to
[m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon]
, and it never leaves
\Omega_{k}
before
T_{\text{return}}^{(k)}
.

Lemma G.1
.

There exists some
c\in(0,\infty)
such that for any
k\in[n_{\text{min}}]
, the following claim holds for all
\epsilon>0
small enough:

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\inf_{y\in[-L,L]:\ y\in(s_{k-1}+\epsilon,s_{k}-\epsilon)}\mathbb{P}_{y}\Big(X^{\eta}_{n}\in\Omega_{k}\ \forall n\in\big[T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big],\ T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\leq\frac{c\log(1/\epsilon)}{\eta}\Big)=1.

During the second phase,
X^{\eta}_{n}
starts from somewhere in
[m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon]
and tries to escape from
\Omega_{k}
, meaning that the phase ends at
\sigma_{k}(\eta)
. During this phase, we show that the distributions of the first exit time
\sigma_{k}(\eta)
and the location
X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}
do converge to the ones described in Theorem
1
as learning rate
\eta
tends to
0
.

Lemma G.2
.

There exist constants
q_{i}>0\ \forall i\in[n_{\text{min}}]
and
q_{i,j}\geq 0\ \forall j\in[n_{\text{min}}]
with
i,j\neq i
such that the following claim holds: given any
C>0,u>0
and any
k,l\in[n_{\text{min}}]
with
k\neq l
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\sup_{x\in[-L,L],\ x\in(m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon)}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C+\exp\big(-(1-C)u\big)

(G.16)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\inf_{x\in[-L,L],\ x\in(m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon)}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u\Big)

\displaystyle\geq-C+\exp\big(-(1+C)u\big)

(G.17)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\sup_{x\in[-L,L],\ x\in(m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon)}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{q_{k,l}+C}{q_{k}}

(G.18)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\inf_{x\in[-L,L],\ x\in(m_{k}-2\epsilon,m_{k}+2\epsilon)}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{q_{k,l}-C}{q_{k}}

(G.19)

for all
\epsilon>0
that are sufficiently small.

Now we are ready to show Theorem
1
.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Fix some
k\in[n_{\text{min}}]
and
x\in\Omega_{k}\cap[-L,L]
. Let
q_{k}
and
q_{k,l}
be the constants in Lemma
G.2
.

We first prove the weak convergence claim in Theorem
1
(i). Arbitrarily choose some
u>0
and
C\in(0,1)
. It suffices to show that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u)

\displaystyle\leq 2C+\exp\big(-(1-C)u\big),

\displaystyle\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u)

\displaystyle\geq(1-C)\Big(-C+\exp\big(-(1+C)u\big)\Big).

Recall the definition of the stopping time
T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}
in
eq.

G.15
. Define event

\displaystyle A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\Big\{X^{\eta}_{n}\in\Omega_{k}\ \forall n\in\big[T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big],\ T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\leq\frac{c\log(1/\epsilon)}{\eta}\Big\}

where
c<\infty
is the constant in Lemma
G.1
. First, since
x\in\Omega_{k}=(s_{k-1},s_{k})
, it holds for all
\epsilon>0
small enough that
x\in(s_{k-1}+\epsilon,s_{k}-\epsilon)
. Next, one can find some
\epsilon>0
such that

•

(Due to Lemma
G.1
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big((A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))^{c}\big)\leq C\ \ \forall\eta\text{ sufficiently small};

•

(Due to
eq.

G.16
eq.

G.17
and strong Markov property) For all
\eta
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\big(\sigma(\eta)-T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big)>(1-C)u\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C+\exp\big(-(1-C)u\big),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\big(\sigma(\eta)-T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big)>u\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq-C+\exp\big(-(1+C)u\big).

Fix such
\epsilon
. Lastly, for this fixed
\epsilon
, due to
\lambda_{k}\in\mathcal{RV}_{-1-l^{*}_{k}(\alpha-1)}(\eta)
and
\alpha>1
, we have
q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\cdot\frac{c\log(1/\epsilon)}{\eta}<Cu
for all
\eta
sufficiently small. In summary, for all
\eta
sufficiently small, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big((A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))^{c}\big)+\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(\big\{q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u\big\}\cap A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C+\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(\big\{q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u\big\}\cap A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C+\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\big(\sigma(\eta)-T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big)>u-q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)\cdot\mathbb{P}_{x}(A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C+\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\big(\sigma(\eta)-T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big)>(1-C)u\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2C+\exp\big(-(1-C)u\big)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(\big\{q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\sigma_{k}(\eta)>u\big\}\cap A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\big(\sigma(\eta)-T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big)>u-q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)\cdot\mathbb{P}_{x}(A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\big(\sigma(\eta)-T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big)>u-q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)\cdot(1-C)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(q_{k}\lambda_{k}(\eta)\big(\sigma(\eta)-T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\big)>u\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)\cdot(1-C)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(1-C)\Big(-C+\exp\big(-(1+C)u\big)\Big)

so this concludes the proof for Theorem
1
(i).

In order to prove claims in Theorem
1
(ii), we first observe that on event
A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)
we must have
\sigma(\eta)>T^{(k)}_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)
. Next, arbitrarily choose some
C\in(0,1)
, and note that it suffices to show that
\mathbb{P}_{x}(X^{\eta}_{\sigma(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l})\in\Big((1-C)\frac{q_{k,l}-C}{q_{k}},C+\frac{q_{k,l}+C}{q_{k}}\Big)
holds for all
\eta
sufficiently small. Again, we can find
\epsilon>0
such that

•

(Due to Lemma
G.1
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\big((A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))^{c}\big)\leq C\ \ \forall\eta\text{ sufficiently small};

•

(Due to
eq.

G.18
eq.

G.19
and strong Markov property) For all
\eta
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\frac{q_{k,l}-C}{q_{k}}\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)\leq\frac{q_{k,l}+C}{q_{k}}.

In summary, for all
\eta
sufficiently small, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{x}\big((A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))^{c}\big)+\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(\big\{X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\big\}\cap A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C+\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)\mathbb{P}_{x}(A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))

\displaystyle\leq C+\frac{q_{k,l}+C}{q_{k}}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(\big\{X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\big\}\cap A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{\sigma_{k}(\eta)}\in\Omega_{l}\ \Big|\ A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon)\Big)\mathbb{P}_{x}(A_{k}(\eta,\epsilon))

\displaystyle\geq(1-C)\frac{q_{k,l}-C}{q_{k}}

and this concludes the proof.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to the proofs of Lemma
G.1
and Lemma
G.2
. Specifically, Lemma
G.1
is an immediate Corollary of Lemma
G.13
, the proof of which will be provided below. The proof of Lemma
G.2
can be found at the end of this section.

G.1
Proofs of Lemma
G.1
,
G.2

The following three lemmas will be applied repeatedly throughout this section. The proofs are straightforward but provided in Section
K
for the sake of completeness.

Lemma G.3
.

Given two real functions
a:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
b:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
a(\epsilon)\downarrow 0,b(\epsilon)\downarrow 0
as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, and a family of geometric RVs
\{U(\epsilon):\epsilon>0\}
with success rate
a(\epsilon)
(namely,
\mathbb{P}(U(\epsilon)>k)=(1-a(\epsilon))^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
), for any
c>1
, there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
,

\exp\Big(-\frac{c\cdot a(\epsilon)}{b(\epsilon)}\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(U(\epsilon)>\frac{1}{b(\epsilon)}\Big)\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{a(\epsilon)}{c\cdot b(\epsilon)}\Big).

Lemma G.4
.

Given two real functions
a:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
b:\mathbb{R}_{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
a(\epsilon)\downarrow 0,b(\epsilon)\downarrow 0
and

a(\epsilon)/b(\epsilon)\rightarrow 0

as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, and a family of geometric RVs
\{U(\epsilon):\epsilon>0\}
with success rate
a(\epsilon)
(namely,
\mathbb{P}(U(\epsilon)>k)=(1-a(\epsilon))^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
), for any
c>1
there exists some
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
,

\displaystyle a(\epsilon)/(c\cdot b(\epsilon))\leq\mathbb{P}(U(\epsilon)\leq 1/b(\epsilon))\leq c\cdot a(\epsilon)/b(\epsilon)

Lemma G.5
.

Suppose that a function
g:E\mapsto\mathbb{R}
(where
E
is an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
) is
g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(E)
and
\left\lVert\nabla^{2}g(\cdot)\right\rVert\leq C
on its domain
E
for some constant
C<\infty
.
For a finite integer
n
, a sequence of vectors
\{z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and vectors
x,\widetilde{x}\in E,\eta>0
, consider two sequences
\{x_{k}\}_{k=0,\ldots,n}
and
\{\widetilde{x}_{k}\}_{k=0,\ldots,n}
constructed as follows:

\displaystyle x_{0}

\displaystyle=x

\displaystyle x_{k}

\displaystyle=x_{k-1}+\eta\nabla g(x_{k-1})+\eta z_{k}\ \ \ \forall k=1,2,\cdots,n

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{0}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{x}

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{k}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{x}_{k-1}+\eta\nabla g(\widetilde{x}_{k-1})\ \ \ \forall k=1,2,\cdots,n

If we have that the line segment from
x_{k}
to
\widetilde{x}_{k}
is contained in
E
and
\eta\left\lVert z_{1}+\cdots+z_{k}\right\rVert+\left\lVert x-\widetilde{x}\right\rVert\leq\widetilde{c}
for all
k=1,2,\cdots,n
for some
\widetilde{c}>0
, then

\displaystyle\left\lVert x_{k}-\widetilde{x}_{k}\right\rVert\leq\widetilde{c}\cdot\exp(\eta Ck)\ \ \ \forall k=1,2,\cdots,n.

To facilitate the analysis below, we introduce some additional notations.
First, we will group the noises
Z_{n}
based on a threshold level
\delta>0
: let us define

\displaystyle Z_{n}^{\leq\delta,\eta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\eta|Z_{n}|\leq\delta\},

(G.20)

\displaystyle Z_{n}^{>\delta,\eta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\eta|Z_{n}|>\delta\}.

(G.21)

The former are viewed as
small noises
while the latter will be referred to as
large noises
or
large jumps
.
Furthermore, for any
j\geq 1
, define the
j
th
arrival time and size of large jumps as

\displaystyle T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n>T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta):\ \eta|Z_{n}|>\delta\},\quad T_{0}^{\eta}(\delta)=0

(G.22)

\displaystyle W^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)}.

(G.23)

Next, for any
\epsilon>0
, let
\Omega_{i}(\epsilon)=[m_{i}-\epsilon,m_{i}+\epsilon]
be an
\epsilon-
neighborhood of the local minimum
m_{i}
, and
S_{i}(\epsilon)=[s_{i}-\epsilon,s_{i}+\epsilon]
be an
\epsilon-
neighborhood of the local maximum
s_{i}
.

For most part of this section, we will zoom in on one of the local minima
m_{i}
and its attraction field
\Omega_{i}=(s_{i-1},s_{i})
. Without loss of generality, we assume
m_{i}=0
, and denote the attraction field as
\Omega=(s_{-},s_{+})
. (If
m_{i}
happens to be the local minimum at the left or right boundary, then the attraction field is
[-L,s_{+})
or
(s_{-},L]
where the SGD iterates will be reflected at
\pm L
.) Henceforth we will drop the dependency on notation
i
when referring to this specific attraction field until the very end of this section. Throughout the proof, the following (deterministic) dynamic systems will be used frequently as benchmark processes to indicate the most likely location of the SGD iterates. Specifically, given any
x\in\Omega
, we use
X_{n}(x)
to indicate that the starting point is
x
, namely
X_{0}(x)=x
. Similarly, consider the following ODE
\textbf{x}^{\eta}(t;x)
as

\displaystyle\textbf{x}^{\eta}(0;x)

\displaystyle=x;

(G.24)

\displaystyle\frac{d\textbf{x}^{\eta}(t;x)}{dt}

\displaystyle=-\eta f^{\prime}\Big(\textbf{x}^{\eta}(t;x)\Big).

(G.25)

When we use update rate
\eta=1
, we will drop the dependency of
\eta
and simply use
\textbf{x}(t;x)
to denote the process.

Based on Assumption 3, we know the existence of some constant
\bar{\epsilon}\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
(note that
\epsilon_{0}
is the constant in
eq.

G.8
) such that

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{-s_{-},s_{+}\},

(G.26)

\displaystyle l^{*}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\lceil r/b\rceil,

(G.27)

\displaystyle(l^{*}-1)b+100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon}

\displaystyle<r-100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon}

(G.28)

\displaystyle r+100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon}

\displaystyle<l^{*}b-100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon}.

(G.29)

Here
r
can be understood as the effective
radius
of the said attraction field. Also, we fix such
\bar{\epsilon}
small enough so that (let
c_{-}^{L}=-f^{\prime}(-L),c_{+}^{L}=-f^{\prime}(-L)
), we have

\displaystyle 0.9c_{-}^{L}\leq-f^{\prime}(x)\leq 1.1c_{-}^{L}\ \

\displaystyle\forall x\in[-L,-L+100\bar{\epsilon}],

(G.30)

\displaystyle 0.9c_{+}^{L}\geq-f^{\prime}(x)\geq 1.1c_{+}^{L}\ \

\displaystyle\forall x\in[L-100\bar{\epsilon},L].

(G.31)

Similar to the definition of ODE
\textbf{x}^{\eta}
, let us consider the following construction of ODE
\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}
that can be understood as
\textbf{x}^{\eta}
perturbed by
l^{*}
shocks. Specifically, consider a sequence or real numbers
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}<\cdots<t_{l^{*}}
and real numbers
w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}}
where
|w_{j}|\leq b
for each
j
. Let
\textbf{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l^{*}}),\textbf{w}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}})
. Based on these two sequences and rate
\eta>0
, define
\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t;x)
as

\displaystyle\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(0,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{L}(x+\varphi_{b}(w_{1}));

(G.32)

\displaystyle\frac{d\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})}{dt}

\displaystyle=-\eta f^{\prime}\big(\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})\big)\ \ \ \forall t\notin\{t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{l^{*}}\}

(G.33)

\displaystyle\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t_{j},x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{L}\big(\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t_{j}-,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})+\varphi_{b}(w_{j})\big)\ \ \ \forall j=2,\cdots,l^{*}

(G.34)

Again, when
\eta=1
we drop the notational dependency on
\eta
and use
\tilde{\textbf{x}}
to denote the process. Now from
eq.

G.28
eq.

G.29
one can easily see the following fact: there exist constants
\bar{t},\bar{\delta}>0
such that
\tilde{\textbf{x}}(t_{l^{*}},0;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})\notin\Omega
(note that the starting point is
0
, the local minimum) only if (under the condition that
|w_{j}|\leq b\ \ \forall j
)

\displaystyle t_{j}-t_{j-1}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{t}\ \ \forall j=2,3,\cdots,l^{*}

(G.35)

\displaystyle|w_{j}|

\displaystyle>\bar{\delta}\ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,l^{*}.

(G.36)

The intuition is as follows: if the inter-arrival time between any of the
l^{*}
jumps is too long, then the path of
\tilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t;x)
will drift back to the local minimum
m_{i}
so that the remaining
l^{*}-1
shocks (whose sizes are bounded by
b
) cannot overcome the radius
r
which is strictly larger than
(l^{*}-1)b
; similarly, if size of any of the shocks is too small, then since all other jumps have sizes bounded by
b
, the shock created by the
l^{*}_{i}
jumps will be smaller than
(l^{*}-1)b+100\bar{\epsilon}
, which is strictly less than
r
.
We fix these constants
\bar{t},\bar{\delta}
throughout the analysis, and stress again that their values are dictated by the geometry of the function
f
, thus
do not
vary with the accuracy parameters
\epsilon
and
\delta
mentioned earlier. In particular, choose
\bar{\delta}
such that
\bar{\delta}<\bar{\epsilon}
.

In our analysis below,
\epsilon>0
will be a variable representing the level of
accuracy
in our analysis. For instance, for small
\epsilon
, the chance that SGD iterates will visit somewhere that is
\epsilon-
close to
s_{-}
or
s_{+}
(namely, the boundary of the attraction filed) should be small. Consider some
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
where
\epsilon_{0}
is the constant in Assumption 1. Due to
eq.

G.8
eq.

G.9
, one can see the existence of some
g_{0}>0,c_{1}<\infty
such that

•

|f^{\prime}(x)|\geq g_{0}
for any
x\in\Omega
such that
|x-s_{-}|>\epsilon_{0},||x-s_{+}|>\epsilon_{0}
;

•

Let
\hat{t}_{\text{ODE}}(x,\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{t\geq 0:\ \textbf{x}^{\eta}(t,x)\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]\}
be the time that the ODE returns to a
\epsilon-
neighborhood of local minimum of
\Omega
when starting from
x
. As proved in Lemma 3.5 of
Pavlyukevich 2005
, for any
x\in\Omega
such that
|x-s_{-}|>\epsilon,|x-s_{+}|>\epsilon
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{t}_{\text{ODE}}(x,\eta)\leq c_{1}\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)}{\eta}

(G.37)

and we define the function

\displaystyle\hat{t}(\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}c_{1}\log(1/\epsilon).

(G.38)

In short, given any accuracy level
\epsilon
, the results above give us an upper bound for how fast the ODE would return to a neighborhood of the local minimum, if the starting point is not too close to the boundary of this attraction field
\Omega
.

For the first few technical results established below, we show that, without large jumps, the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}(x)
are unlikely to show significant deviation from the deterministic gradient descent process
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(x)
defined as

\displaystyle\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=x,

(G.39)

\displaystyle\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)-\eta f^{\prime}\Big(\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)\Big).

(G.40)

We are ready to state the first lemma, where we bound the distance between the gradient descent iterates
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(y)
and the ODE
\textbf{x}^{\eta}(t,x)
when the initial conditions
x,y
are close enough.

Lemma G.6
.

The following claim holds for all
\eta>0
: for any
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y)|\leq(2\eta M+|x-y|)\exp(\eta Mt)

where
M\in(0,\infty)
is the constant in
eq.

G.10
.

Proof.

Define a continuous-time process
\textbf{y}^{\eta}(s;y)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y)
, and note that

\displaystyle\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\textbf{x}^{\eta}(\lfloor s\rfloor,x)-\eta\int_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{s}f^{\prime}(\textbf{x}^{\eta}(u,x))du

\displaystyle\textbf{x}^{\eta}(\lfloor s\rfloor,x)

\displaystyle=x-\eta\int_{0}^{\lfloor s\rfloor}f^{\prime}(\textbf{x}^{\eta}(u,x))du

\displaystyle\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y)=\textbf{y}^{\eta}(\lfloor s\rfloor,y)

\displaystyle=y-\eta\int_{0}^{\lfloor s\rfloor}f^{\prime}(\textbf{y}^{\eta}(u,y))du.

Therefore, if we define function

b(u)=\textbf{x}^{\eta}(u,x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}(u,y),

from the fact
|f^{\prime}(\cdot)|\leq M
, one can see that
|b(u)|\leq\eta M+|x-y|
for any
u\in[0,1)
and
|b(1)|\leq 2\eta M+|x-y|
. In case that
s>1
, from the display above and the fact
|f^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)|\leq M
, we now have

\displaystyle|\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(x)-\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)|

\displaystyle\leq|b({\lfloor s\rfloor})|+\eta M;

\displaystyle|b({\lfloor s\rfloor})|

\displaystyle\leq\eta M\int_{1}^{\lfloor s\rfloor}|b(u)|du.

From Gronwall’s inequality (see Theorem 68, Chapter V of
Protter 2005
, where we let function
\alpha(u)
be
\alpha(u)=|b(u+1)|
), we have

|\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(x)-\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)|\leq(2\eta M+|x-y|)\exp(\eta Mt).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Now we consider an extension of the previous Lemma in the following sense: we add perturbations to the gradient descent process and ODE, and show that, when both perturbed by
l^{*}
similar perturbations, the ODE and gradient descent process should still stay close enough. Analogous to the definition of the perturbed ODE
\widetilde{x}^{\eta}
in
eq.

G.32
-
eq.

G.34
, we can construct a process
\widetilde{Y}^{\eta}
as a perturbed gradient descent process as follows. For a sequence of integers
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots<t_{l^{*}}
(let
\textbf{t}=(t_{j})_{j\geq 1}
) and a sequence of real numbers
\widetilde{w}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{w}_{l^{*}}
(let
\widetilde{\textbf{w}}=(\widetilde{w}_{j})_{j\geq 1}
) and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, define (for all
n=1,2,\cdots,t_{l^{*}}
) the perturbed gradient descent iterates with gradient clipping at
b
and reflection at
\pm L
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{n}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{L}\Big(\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{n-1}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})+\varphi_{b}\big(-\eta f^{\prime}(\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{n-1}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}}))+\sum_{j=2}^{l^{*}}\mathbbm{1}\{n=t_{j}\}\widetilde{w}_{j}\big)\Big)

(G.41)

with initial condition
\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{0}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})=\varphi_{L}\big(y+\varphi_{b}(\widetilde{w}_{1})\big)
.

Corollary G.7
.

Given any
\epsilon>0
, the following claim holds for all sufficiently small
\eta>0
: for any
x,y\in\Omega
, and sequence of integers
\textbf{t}=(t_{j})_{j=1}^{l^{*}}
and any two sequence of real numbers
\textbf{w}=(w_{j})_{j=1}^{l^{*}},\widetilde{\textbf{w}}=(\widetilde{w}_{j})_{j\geq 1}^{l^{*}}
such that

•

|x-y|<\epsilon
;

•

t_{1}=0
, and
t_{j}-t_{j-1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta
for all
j\geq 1
where
\bar{t}
is the constant in
eq.

G.35
;

•

|w_{j}-\widetilde{w}_{j}|<\epsilon
for all
j\geq 1
;

then we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,t_{l^{*}}]}|\widetilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})-\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor t\rfloor}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})|\leq\bar{\rho}\epsilon

where the constant
\bar{\rho}=(3\exp(\eta M\bar{t})+3)^{l^{*}}
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, fix some
\eta\in(0,\epsilon/2M)
. We will show that for any
\eta
in the range the claim would hold.

First, on interval
[0,t_{2})
, from Lemma
G.6
, one can see that (since
2M\eta<\epsilon
)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,t_{2})}|\widetilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})-\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor t\rfloor}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})|\leq 3\exp(\eta M\bar{t})\cdot\epsilon.

The at
t=t_{2}
, by considering the difference between
w_{2}
and
\widetilde{w}_{2}
, and the possible change due to one more gradient descent step (which is bounded by
\eta M<\epsilon
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,t_{2}]}|\widetilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})-\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor t\rfloor}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})|\leq(3\exp(\eta M\bar{t})+2)\cdot\epsilon.

Now we proceed inductively. For any
j=2,3,\cdots,l^{*}-1
, assume that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,t_{j}]}|\widetilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})-\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor t\rfloor}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})|\leq(3\exp(\eta M\bar{t})+3)^{j-1}\cdot\epsilon.

Then by focusing on interval
[t_{j},t_{j+1}]
and using Lemma
G.6
again, one can show that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{j},t_{j-1}]}|\widetilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}(t,x;\textbf{t},\textbf{w})-\widetilde{\textbf{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor t\rfloor}(y;\textbf{t},\widetilde{\textbf{w}})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon+\big((3\exp(\eta M\bar{t})+3)^{j-1}+1\big)\exp(\eta M\bar{t})\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq(3\exp(\eta M\bar{t})+3)^{j}\cdot\epsilon.

This concludes the proof.
∎

In the next few results, we show that the same can be said for gradient descent iterates
\widetilde{\textbf{y}}_{n}
and the SGD iterates
X_{n}
. Specifically, our first goal is to show that before any
large
jump (see the definition in
G.21
), it is unlikely that the gradient descent process
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}
would deviate too far from
X^{\eta}_{n}
. Define the event

\displaystyle A(n,\eta,\epsilon,\delta)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\max_{k=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)}\eta|Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{k}|\leq\epsilon\Big\}

(G.42)

and recall that arrival times
T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)
are defined in
eq.

G.22
.

As a building block, we first study the case when the starting point
x
is close to the reflection boundary
-L
. The takeaway from the next result is that the reflection operator hardly comes into play, since the SGD iterates would most likely quickly move to somewhere far enough from
\pm L
; besides, throughout this procedure the SGD iterates would most likely stay pretty close to the corresponding deterministic gradient descent process.

Lemma G.8
.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon}/9)
, it holds for any sufficiently small
\epsilon,\delta,\eta>0
that, if
x\in[-L,-L+\bar{\epsilon}]
and
\rho_{0}(|x-y|+9\epsilon)<\bar{\epsilon}
, then on event
A(n,\eta,\epsilon,\delta)
we have

\displaystyle|X^{\eta}_{k}(x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{k}(y)|\leq\rho_{0}\cdot(|x-y|+9\epsilon)\ \ \forall k=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)\wedge\widetilde{T}_{\text{escape}}^{\eta}(x)

where
\widetilde{T}_{\text{escape}}^{\eta}(x)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n\geq 0:\ X^{\eta}_{n}(x)>-L+\bar{\epsilon}\}
and
\rho_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\exp\Big(\frac{2M\bar{\epsilon}}{0.9c^{L}_{-}}\Big)
is a constant that does not vary with our choice of
\epsilon,\delta,\eta
.

Proof.

For any
k<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)
, we know that
Z_{k}=Z^{\leq\delta}_{k}
(thus
\eta|Z_{k}|<\delta
). Also, recall that
|f^{\prime}(x)|\leq M
for any
x\in\Omega_{i}
. Therefore, as long as
\eta
and
\delta
are small enough, we will have that

\displaystyle|\eta(-f^{\prime}(X^{\eta}_{n}(x))+Z^{\leq\delta}_{k})|\leq b

(G.43)

so the gradient clipping operator in
eq.

G.12
has no effect when
k<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)
, and in fact the only possible time for the gradient clipping trick to work is at
T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)
. Therefore, we can safely rewrite the SGD update as

\displaystyle X^{\eta}_{k}(x)=X^{\eta}_{k-1}(x)-\eta f^{\prime}(X^{\eta}_{k-1}(x))+\eta Z_{k}+R_{k}\ \ \ \forall k<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)

where each
R_{k}\geq 0
and it represents the push caused by reflection at
-L
.

First, choose
\epsilon
small enough so that
9\epsilon<\bar{\epsilon}
. Next, based on
eq.

G.31
we have the following lower bound:

\displaystyle X^{\eta}_{k}(x)\geq x+0.9c^{L}_{-}\eta k-\epsilon\ \ \ \forall k<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta).

Let
\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n\geq 0:X^{\eta}_{n}(x)\geq-L+2\epsilon\}
. Due to the inequality above, we know that

\displaystyle\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)\leq\frac{3\epsilon}{0.9c^{L}_{-}\eta}.

(G.44)

One the other hand, given the current choice of
\epsilon
, if we choose
\eta
and
\delta
small enough, then using the same argument leading to
eq.

G.43
, we will have

\displaystyle X^{\eta}_{\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)}(x)\leq-L+2.1\epsilon\leq x+1.1c^{L}_{-}\eta k+2.1\epsilon

if
\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)\geq 1
(namely
x<-L+2\epsilon
).

Let us inspect the two scenarios separately. First, assume
\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)\geq 1
. For the deterministic gradient descent process
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(y)
, we have the following bounds:

\displaystyle y+0.9c^{L}_{-}\eta k\leq\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{k}(y)\leq y+1.1c^{L}_{-}\eta k\ \ \forall k\leq\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1).

This gives us

\displaystyle|X^{\eta}_{k}(x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{k}(y)|\leq|x-y|+0.2c^{L}_{-}\eta k+2.1\epsilon\ \ \forall k\leq\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1).

At time
k=\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)
, due to previous bound on
\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)
, we know that
|X^{\eta}_{\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)}(x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)}(y)|\leq|x-y|+7\epsilon
. If
n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)\leq\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)
then we have already shown the desired claim. Otherwise, starting from time
\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)
, due to the definition of event
A(n,\eta,\epsilon,\delta)
in
eq.

G.42
, we know that the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}(x)
will not touch the boundary
-L
afterwards. Therefore, by directly applying Lemma
G.5
, and notice that
|f^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leq M
for any
x\in[-L,L]
and, we have

\displaystyle|X^{\eta}_{k}(x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{k}(y)|\leq(|x-y|+9\epsilon)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{2M\bar{\epsilon}}{0.9c^{L}_{-}}\Big)\ \ \ \forall k\leq n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)\wedge\widetilde{T}_{\text{escape}}^{\eta}(x).

Indeed, it suffices to use Lemma
G.5
for the next
\lceil 2\bar{\epsilon}/(0.9\eta c^{L}_{-})\rceil
steps to show that
|X^{\eta}_{k}(x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{k}(y)|
should be smaller than
\epsilon
for the next
\lceil 2\bar{\epsilon}/(0.9\eta c^{L}_{-})\rceil
steps, while
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{k}(y)
will reach some where in
(-L+2\bar{\epsilon},-L+3\bar{\epsilon})
within
\lceil 2\bar{\epsilon}/(0.9\eta c^{L}_{-})\rceil
steps so we must have

\displaystyle n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)\wedge\widetilde{T}_{\text{escape}}^{\eta}(x)\wedge\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)-\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)\leq 2\bar{\epsilon}/(0.9\eta c^{L}_{-})

(G.45)

Lastly, in the case
\widetilde{t}_{0}(x,\epsilon)=0
(which means
x\geq-L+\epsilon
), we can use Lemma
G.5
directly as we did above and establish the same bound. This concludes the proof.
∎

Obviously, a similar result can be shown if
x
is in the rightmost attraction field
(s_{n_{\text{min}-1}},L]
and the approach is identical. We omit the details here. In the next Lemma, we consider the scenario where the starting point
x
is far enough from the boundaries.

Lemma G.9
.

Given any
\epsilon>0
, the following holds for all sufficiently small
\eta>0
: for any
x,y\in\Omega
and positive integer
n
such that
|x-L|>2\epsilon,|x+L|>2\epsilon,|x-s_{-}|>2\epsilon,|x-s_{+}|>2\epsilon
and
|x-y|<\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta Mn)}
, on event

\displaystyle A(n,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta Mn)},\delta)\cap\Big\{|\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{j}(y)|\in\Omega,|X^{\eta}_{j}(x)|\in\Omega\ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)\Big\}

we have

|\widetilde{X}^{\eta}_{m}(y)-X^{\eta}_{m}(x)|\leq\epsilon\ \ \forall m=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1).

Proof.

For sufficiently small
\eta
, we will have that the (deterministic) gradient descent iterates
|\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}|
is monotonically decreasing in
n
, which ensures that
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}
always stays in the range that are at least
\epsilon-
away from
\pm L
or
s_{-},s_{+}
. We now show that the claim holds for any such
\eta
.

On event
\Big\{|\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{j}(y)|\in\Omega,|X^{\eta}_{j}(x)|\in\Omega\ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)\Big\}
, we are able to apply Lemma
G.5
inductively for any
m\in[n]
and obtain that

\displaystyle|\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{j}(y)-X^{\eta}_{j}(x)|\leq(|x-y|+\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta Mn)})\exp(\eta Mj)<\epsilon\ \ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,m

and conclude the proof. The reason to apply the Lemma inductively for
m=1,2,\cdots,n
, instead of directly at step
n
, is to ensure that SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}
would not hit the boundary
\pm L
(so the reflection operator would not come into play on the time interval we are currently interested in), thus ensuring that Lemma
G.5
is applicable.
∎

Similar to the extension from Lemma
G.6
to Corollary
G.7
, we can extend Lemma
G.9
to show that, if we consider the a gradient descent process that is only perturbed by large noises, then it should stay pretty close to the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}
. To be specific, let

\displaystyle Y^{\eta}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=x

(G.46)

\displaystyle Y^{\eta}_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{L}\Big(Y^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)-\varphi_{b}\big(-\eta f^{\prime}\big(Y^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)\big)+\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbbm{1}\{n=T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)\}\eta Z_{n}\big)\Big).

(G.47)

be a gradient descent process (with gradient clipping at threshold
b
) that is only shocked by large noises in
(Z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
. The next corollary can be shown by an approach that is identical to Corollary
G.7
(namely, inductively repeating Lemma
G.9
at each jump time) so we omit the details here.

Corollary G.10
.

Given any
\epsilon>0
, the following holds for any sufficiently small
\eta>0
: For any
|x|<2\epsilon
, on event
A_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta)\cap B_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta)
, we have

\displaystyle|Y^{\eta}_{n}(x)-X^{\eta}_{n}(x)|<\widetilde{\rho}\epsilon\ \ \forall n=1,2,\cdots,T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)

where

\displaystyle A_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\Big\{\forall i=1,\cdots,l^{*},\ \max_{j=T^{\eta}_{i-1}(\delta)+1,\cdots,T^{\eta}_{i}(\delta)-1}\eta|Z_{T^{\eta}_{i-1}(\delta)+1}+\cdots+Z_{j}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(2\bar{t}M)}\Big\};

\displaystyle B_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\Big\{\forall j=2,\cdots,l^{*},T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)-T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta\Big\}

and
\widetilde{\rho}\in(0,\infty)
is a constant that does not vary with
\eta,\delta,\epsilon
.

The next two results shows that the type of events
A(n,\eta,\epsilon,\delta)
defined in
eq.

G.42
is indeed very likely to occur, especially for small
\epsilon
. For clarity of the presentation, we introduce the following definitions that are slightly more general than the
small
and
large
jumps defined in
eq.

G.20
eq.

G.21
(for any
c>0
)

\displaystyle Z^{\leq c}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{|Z_{n}|\leq c\},

\displaystyle Z^{>c}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{|Z_{n}|>c\}.

Lemma G.11
.

Define functions
u(\eta)=\delta/\eta^{1-\Delta},\ v(\eta)=\epsilon\eta^{\widetilde{\Delta}}
with
\epsilon,\delta>0
. If real numbers
\Delta,\widetilde{\Delta},\beta,\epsilon,\delta
and positive integers
j,N
are such that the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\in\big[0,(1-\frac{1}{\alpha})\wedge\frac{1}{2}\big),

(G.48)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\in\big(1,(2-2\Delta)\wedge\alpha(1-\Delta)\big),

(G.49)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}

\displaystyle\in[0,\frac{\Delta}{2}],\ \widetilde{\Delta}<\alpha(1-\Delta)-\beta,

(G.50)

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle<\big(\alpha(1-\Delta)-\beta\big)j,

(G.51)

\displaystyle v(\eta)-j\eta u(\eta)

\displaystyle\geq v(\eta)/2\ \ \ \text{for all $\eta>0$ sufficiently small},

(G.52)

then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{k=1,2,\cdots,\lceil 1/\eta^{\beta}\rceil}\eta\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{k}\big|>3v(\eta)\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

as
\eta\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

From the stated range of the parameters, we know that

\displaystyle\alpha(1-\Delta)

\displaystyle>\beta,

\displaystyle\big(\alpha(1-\Delta)-\beta\big)j

\displaystyle>N,

so we are able to find
\gamma\in(0,1)
small enough such that

\displaystyle\alpha(1-\Delta)(1-2\gamma)

\displaystyle>\beta,

(G.53)

\displaystyle\big(\alpha(1-\Delta)(1-2\gamma)-\beta\big)j

\displaystyle>N.

(G.54)

Fix such
\gamma\in(0,1)
for the rest of the proof, and let
n(\eta)\triangleq\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil
,
I\triangleq\#\Big\{i\in[n(\eta)]:|Z_{i}^{\leq u(\eta)}|>u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}\Big\}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{n(\eta)}\big|>v(\eta)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{n(\eta)}\big|>v(\eta),\ I=i\Big)}_{\triangleq\text{(I)}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{n(\eta)}\big|>v(\eta),\ I\geq j\Big)}_{\triangleq\text{(II)}}

Note that since
|Z_{i}^{\leq u(\eta)}|<u(\eta)
,

(I)

\displaystyle\leq{n(\eta)\choose i}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{n(\eta)-i}\big|>\frac{v(\eta)-i\eta u(\eta)}{\eta},|Z_{i}^{\leq u(\eta)}|\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}\ \forall i\in[n(\eta)-i]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq n(\eta)^{i}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{n(\eta)-i}\big|>\frac{v(\eta)-i\eta u(\eta)}{\eta}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq n(\eta)^{i}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{n(\eta)-i}\big|>\frac{v(\eta)}{2\eta}\Big)

(G.55)

where the last inequality follows from
eq.

G.52
. First, since
\mathbb{E}Z_{1}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{1}\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\mathbb{E}Z^{>u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{1}\big|

\displaystyle=\int_{u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(|Z_{1}|>x)dx\in\mathcal{RV}_{(\alpha-1)(1-\gamma)(1-\Delta)}(\eta).

Therefore, for all
\eta>0
that are sufficiently small,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{1}+\cdots+\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{n(\eta)-i}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle n(\eta)\cdot\eta^{(\alpha-1)(1-\Delta)(1-2\gamma)}\leq 2\eta^{(\alpha-1)(1-\Delta)(1-2\gamma)-\beta}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1/\eta)^{(1-\Delta)(1-2\gamma)}\ \ \ \ \text{due to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{proof lemma bernstein choose gamma 1}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{v(\eta)}{4\eta}\ \ \ \ \text{due to $\widetilde{\Delta}/2\leq\Delta$ in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{proof lemma bernstein parameter 3} and $1-\gamma<1$}.

If we let
Y_{n}=Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{n}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{n}
and plug the bound above back into
eq.

G.55
, then (for all
\eta>0
that are sufficiently small)

(I)

\displaystyle\leq n(\eta)^{i}\cdot\mathbb{P}(|Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{n(\eta)-i}|>\frac{v(\eta)}{4\eta})

\displaystyle\leq n(\eta)^{i}\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{16}\cdot 1/\eta^{2-2\widetilde{\Delta}}}{2\big(n(\eta)-i\big)\mathbb{E}|Y_{1}|^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\delta^{1-\gamma}\cdot(1/\eta)^{(1-\Delta)(1-\gamma)}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{4}/\eta^{1-\widetilde{\Delta}}}\right)

(G.56)

where the last inequality is obtained from Bernstein’s inequality.
Note that from Karamata’s theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|Y_{1}|^{2}

\displaystyle=var(Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{1})\leq\mathbb{E}|Z^{\leq u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}_{1}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}}2x\mathbb{P}(|Z_{1}|>x)dx\in\mathcal{RV}_{-(1-\Delta)(1-\gamma)(2-\alpha)}(\eta).

Now note that

•

In case that
\alpha<2
, for all
\eta>0
that are sufficiently small, we have (using
eq.

G.50
)

\displaystyle 2\big(n(\eta)-i\big)\mathbb{E}|Y_{1}|^{2}\leq(1/\eta)^{\beta+(2-\alpha)(1-\Delta)}<(1/\eta)^{2(1-\Delta)}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{1/\eta^{2-2\widetilde{\Delta}}}{2\big(n(\eta)-i\big)\mathbb{E}|Y_{1}|^{2}}\geq 1/\eta^{\Delta};

•

In case that
\alpha\geq 2
, for all
\eta>0
that are sufficiently small,

\displaystyle 2\big(n(\eta)-i\big)\mathbb{E}|Y_{1}|^{2}<1/\eta^{\beta+\frac{\Delta}{2}}

and we know that
\beta+\frac{\Delta}{2}<2-2\widetilde{\Delta}
due to
2-\beta>2\Delta
and
2\widetilde{\Delta}\leq\Delta
(see
eq.

G.48
-
eq.

G.50
);

•

Since
\gamma>0
and
2\widetilde{\Delta}\leq\Delta
, we know that

\displaystyle(1-\Delta)(1-\gamma)+(1-\widetilde{\Delta})<2-2\widetilde{\Delta}.

Therefore, it is easy to see that the R.H.S. of
eq.

G.56
decays at a geometric rate as
\eta
tends to zero, hence
o(\eta^{N})
. On the other hand,

(II)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(I\geq j)\leq{n(\eta)\choose j}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(|Z_{i}^{\leq u(\eta)}|>u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}\ \forall i=1,\ldots,j\Big)

\displaystyle\leq n(\eta)^{j}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(|Z_{1}^{\leq u(\eta)}|>u(\eta)^{1-\gamma}\Big)^{j},

which is regularly varying w.r.t.
\eta
with index
\big(\alpha(1-\gamma)(1-\Delta)-\beta\big)j
.
Therefore, for all
\eta>0
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\text{(II)}\leq\eta^{\big(\alpha(1-2\gamma)(1-\Delta)-\beta\big)j}<\eta^{N}\ \ \ \ \text{due to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{proof lemma bernstein choose gamma 2}}.

Collecting results above, we have established that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta\big|Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq u(\eta)}_{n(\eta)}\big|>v(\eta)\Big)=o(\eta^{N}).

The conclusion of the lemma now follows from Etemadi’s theorem.
∎

Now consider the following setting. Let us fix some positive integer
N
and
\beta\in(1,2\wedge\alpha)
. Then we can find some positive integer
j
such that
(\alpha-\beta)j>N
. Meanwhile, given any
\epsilon>0
, we will have
\epsilon-j\delta\geq\epsilon/2
for all
\delta>0
sufficiently small. Therefore, by applying Lemma
G.11
with
\Delta=\widetilde{\Delta}=0
(hence
u(\eta)=\delta/\eta,\ v(\eta)=\epsilon
) and
\beta,j,N,\epsilon,\delta
as described here, we immediately get the following result.

Lemma G.12
.

Given any
\beta\in(1,\alpha\wedge 2)
,
\epsilon>0
, and
N>0
, the following holds for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{j=1,2,\cdots,\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil}\eta|Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{j}|>\epsilon\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

as
\eta\downarrow 0
.

Using results and arguments above, we are able to illustrate the typical behavior of the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}
in the following two scenarios. First, we show that, when starting from most parts in the attraction field
\Omega
, the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}

will most likely return to the neighborhood of the local minimum within a short period of time without exiting
\Omega
. Given that there are only finitely many attraction fields on
f
, it is easy to see that the key technical tool Lemma
G.1
follows immediately from the next result.

Lemma G.13
.

For sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, the following claim holds:

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\sup_{x\in\Omega:|x-s_{-}|\wedge|x-s_{+}|>\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{n}\in\Omega\ \forall n\leq T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon),\text{and }T_{\mathrm{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\leq\rho(\epsilon)/\eta\Big)=1

where the stopping time involved is defined as

\displaystyle T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n\geq 0:X^{\eta}_{n}(x)\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]\}

the function
\hat{t}(\epsilon)
is defined in
eq.

G.38
, and the function
\rho(\cdot)
is defined as
\rho(\epsilon)=\frac{3\bar{\epsilon}}{0.9c^{L}_{-}\wedge c^{L}_{+}}+2\hat{t}(\epsilon)

Proof.

Throughout, we only consider
\epsilon
small enough so that Lemma
G.8
could hold. Also, fix some
N>0,\Delta_{\alpha}\in(0,\alpha-1)
and
\beta\in(1,\alpha)
. Let
\sigma(x,\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n\geq 0:X^{\eta}_{n}\notin\Omega\}
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\Omega=[-L,s_{+})
and
x<0
(so reflection at
-L
) is a possibility. Any other case can be addressed similarly as shown below.

From Lemma
G.4
and the regular varying nature of
H(\cdot)
, we have, for any
\epsilon,\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq\rho(\epsilon)/\eta)\leq\eta^{\alpha-1-\Delta_{\alpha}}

(G.57)

for any sufficiently small
\eta
.

Let
\widetilde{T}^{\eta}_{\text{escape}}(x)
be the stopping time defined in Lemma
G.8
. From
eq.

G.44
,
eq.

G.45
,
eq.

G.57
and Lemma
G.12
, we know that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-L,-L+\bar{\epsilon}]}\mathbb{P}\Big(\widetilde{T}^{\eta}_{\text{escape}}(x)<\sigma(x,\eta),\widetilde{T}^{\eta}_{\text{escape}}(x)\leq\frac{3\bar{\epsilon}}{0.9c^{L}_{-}\eta}\text{ and }X^{\eta}_{\widetilde{T}^{\eta}_{\text{escape}}}(x)\in[-L+\bar{\epsilon},-L+2\bar{\epsilon}]\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-\eta^{N}-\eta^{\alpha-1-\Delta_{\alpha}}

(G.58)

for all sufficiently small
\eta
.

Next, we focus on
x\in\Omega
such that
|x-s_{-}|\wedge|x-s_{+}|>\epsilon
and
x\geq-L+\bar{\epsilon}
. We start by considering the time it took for the (deterministic) gradient descent process
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(x)
to return to
[-1.5\epsilon,1.5\epsilon]
. From the definition of
\hat{t}(\epsilon)
in
eq.

G.38
and Lemma
G.6
, we know that for
\eta
small enough such that
\eta\exp(2M\hat{t}(\epsilon))<0.5\epsilon
, we have

\min\{n\geq 0:\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(x)\in[-1.5\epsilon,1.5\epsilon]\}\leq 2\hat{t}(\epsilon)/\eta.

Now consider event
A(\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{4\exp(2M\hat{t}(\epsilon))},\delta)
(see definition in
eq.

G.42
). From Lemma
G.12
, we know that for any sufficiently small
\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\big(A(\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{4\exp(2M\hat{t}(\epsilon))},\delta)\big)^{c}\Big)=o(\eta^{N}).

(G.59)

Combining this result with
eq.

G.57
eq.

G.59
and Lemma
G.9
, we get

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\Omega:|x-s_{-}|\wedge|x-s_{+}|>\epsilon,x\geq-L+\bar{\epsilon}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)<\sigma(x,\eta),T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\leq 2\hat{t}(\epsilon)/\eta\Big)

(G.60)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\eta^{N}-\eta^{\alpha-1-\Delta_{\alpha}}

(G.61)

for any sufficiently small
\eta
. To conclude the proof, we only to combine strong Markov property (at
\widetilde{T}^{\eta}_{\text{escape}}
) with bounds in
eq.

G.58
eq.

G.61
.
∎

In the next result, we show that, once entering a
\epsilon-
small neighborhood of the local minimum, the SGD iterates will most likely stay there until the next large jump.

Lemma G.14
.

Given
N_{0}>0
, the following claim holds for any
\epsilon,\delta>0
that are sufficiently small:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists n<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\ s.t.\ |X^{\eta}_{n}(x)|>3\epsilon\Big)=o(\eta^{N_{0}})

as
\eta\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

Fix
\epsilon
small enough such that
3\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
(see Assumption 1 for the constant
\epsilon_{0}
). Also, fix some
\Delta\alpha\in(0,1),\beta\in(1,\alpha)
,
N>\alpha+\Delta\alpha-\beta+N_{0}
. Due to Lemma
G.12
, for any
\delta
sufficiently small, we will have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{j=1,2,\cdots,\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil}\eta|Z^{\leq,\delta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq,\delta}_{j}|>\frac{\epsilon}{\exp(2M)}\Big)=o(\eta^{N}).

(G.62)

Fix such
\delta>0
. We now show that the desired claim is true for the chosen
\epsilon,\delta
.

First of all, from Lemma
G.3
, we know the existence of some
\theta>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>1/\eta^{\alpha+\Delta\alpha})=o(\exp(-1/\eta^{\theta})).

(G.63)

Next, let us zoom in on the first
\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil
SGD iterates. For any
\eta
small enough, we will have
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(x)\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]
for any
n\geq 1
and
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil}(x)\in[-\epsilon,\epsilon]
given
x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]
. From now on we only consider such
\eta
. Due to Lemma
G.9
, we know that on event
\big\{\max_{j=1,2,\cdots,\lceil(1/\eta)\rceil}\eta|Z^{\leq,\delta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq,\delta}_{j}|>\frac{\epsilon}{\exp(2M)}\big\}
, we have

\displaystyle|X^{\eta}_{n}(x)|\leq 3\epsilon\ \ \forall n\leq\lceil 1/\eta^{\beta}\rceil\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)

and on event
\big\{\max_{j=1,2,\cdots,\lceil(1/\eta)\rceil}\eta|Z^{\leq,\delta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq,\delta}_{j}|>\frac{\epsilon}{\exp(2M)}\big\}\cap\{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil\}
, we have

X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)}(x)\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]
. Now by repeating the same argument inductively for
\lceil 1/\eta^{\alpha+\Delta\alpha-\beta}\rceil
times, we can show that on event

\displaystyle\{\forall i=1,2,\cdots,\lceil\frac{1}{\eta^{\alpha+\Delta\alpha-\beta}}\rceil,\ \max_{j=1,\cdots,\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil}\eta|Z^{\leq,\delta}_{i\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil+1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq,\delta}_{i\lceil(1/\eta)^{\beta}\rceil+j}|>\frac{\epsilon}{\exp(2M)}\},

we have
|X^{\eta}_{n}(x)|\leq 3\epsilon\ \forall n\leq 1/\eta^{\alpha+\Delta\alpha}\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)
. To conclude the proof, we only need to combine this fact with
eq.

G.62
.
∎

We introduce a few concepts that will be crucial in the analysis below. Recall the definition of perturbed ODE
\widetilde{\textbf{x}}^{\eta}
in
eq.

G.32
-
eq.

G.34

(note that we will drop the notational dependency on learning rate
\eta
when we choose
\eta=1
). Consider the definition of the following two mappings from where
\textbf{w}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}})
is a sequence of real numbers and
\textbf{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},\cdots,t_{l^{*}})
with
0=t_{1}<t_{2}<t_{3}<\cdots
as

\displaystyle h(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\textbf{x}}(t_{l^{*}},0;\textbf{t},\textbf{w}).

Next, define sets (for any
\epsilon\in(-\bar{\epsilon},\bar{\epsilon})
)

\displaystyle E(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\{(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{l^{*}}\times\Big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}:h(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\notin[s_{-}-\epsilon,s_{+}+\epsilon]\}.

(G.64)

We add a few remarks about the two types of sets defined above.

•

Intuitively speaking,
E(\epsilon)

contains all the perturbations (with times and sizes) that can send the ODE
out of the current attraction field (allowing for some error with size
\epsilon
);

•

From the definition of
\bar{t},\bar{\delta}
in
eq.

G.35
eq.

G.36
and Corollary
G.7
, one can easily see that for a fixed
\epsilon\in(-\bar{\epsilon},\bar{\epsilon})
,

\displaystyle(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\in E(\epsilon)\Rightarrow|w_{j}|>\bar{\delta},t_{j}-t_{j-1}\leq\bar{t}\ \ \forall j;

•

Lastly,
E(\epsilon)

are open sets due to
f\in C^{2}
.

Use
\textbf{Leb}_{+}
to denote the Lebesgue measure restricted on
[0,\infty)
, and define (Borel) measure
\nu_{\alpha}
with density on
\mathbb{R}\char 92\relax\{0\}
:

\nu_{\alpha}(dx)=\mathbbm{1}\{x>0\}\frac{\alpha p_{+}}{x^{\alpha+1}}+\mathbbm{1}\{x<0\}\frac{\alpha p_{-}}{|x|^{\alpha+1}}

where
\alpha>1
is the regular variation index for the distribution of
Z_{1}
and
p_{-},p_{+}\in(0,1)
are constants in Assumption 2. Now we can define a Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{l^{*}}\times\Big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}
as product measure

\displaystyle\mu=(\nu_{\alpha})^{l^{*}}\times(\textbf{Leb}_{+})^{l^{*}-1}.

(G.65)

Due to remarks above, one can see that for
\epsilon\in(-\bar{\epsilon},\bar{\epsilon})
, we have
\mu(E(\epsilon))<\infty
. We are now ready to analyze a specific type of noise
Z_{n}
.

Definition G.1
.

For any
n\geq 1
and any
\epsilon\in(-\bar{\epsilon},\bar{\epsilon}),\delta\in(0,b\wedge\bar{\delta}),\eta>0
, we say that the jump
Z_{n}
has
(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)
-overflow
if

•

\eta|Z_{n}|>\delta
;

•

In the set
\{n+1,\cdots,n+2\lceil l^{*}\bar{t}/\eta\rceil\}
, there are at least
(l^{*}-1)
elements (ordered as
n<t_{2}<t_{3}<\cdots<t_{l^{*}}
) such that
\eta|Z_{t_{i}}|>\delta
for any
i=2,\cdots,l^{*}
;

•

Let
t_{1}=n
and
t^{\prime}_{i}=t_{i}-t_{i-1}
for any
i=2,\cdots,l^{*}
,
w_{i}=\eta Z_{i}
for any
i=1,\cdots,l^{*}
, for real sequence
\textbf{w}=(w_{1},w_{2},\cdots,w_{l^{*}})
and a sequence of positive number
\textbf{t}=(\eta(t_{i}-n))_{i=2}^{l^{*}}
, we have

(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\in E(\epsilon).

Moreover, if
Z_{n}
has
(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)-
overflow, then we call
h(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})
as its
(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)-
overflow endpoint
.

Due to the iid nature of
(Z_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, let us consider an iid sequence
(V_{j})_{j\geq 0}
where the sequence has the same law of
Z_{1}
. Note that for any fixed
n\geq 1
, the probability that
Z_{n}
has
(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)
-overflow is equal to the probability that
V_{0}
has
(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)
-overflow. More specifically, we know that
\mathbb{P}(\eta|V_{0}|>\delta)=H(\delta/\eta)
, and now we focus on conditional probability admitting the following form:

\displaystyle p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)=\mathbb{P}\Big(V_{0}\ \text{has $(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)$-overflow}\ \Big|\ \eta|V_{0}|>\delta\Big).

(G.66)

For any open interval
A=(a_{1},a_{2})
such that
A\cap[s_{-}+\bar{\epsilon},s_{+}-\bar{\epsilon}]=\emptyset
, we also define

\displaystyle p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta;A)=\mathbb{P}\Big(V_{0}\ \text{has $(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)$-overflow and the endpoint is in }A\ \Big|\ \eta|V_{0}|>\delta\Big).

(G.67)

Lemma G.15
.

For any
\epsilon\in(-\bar{\epsilon},\bar{\epsilon}),\delta\in(0,b\wedge\bar{\delta})
, and any open interval
A=(a_{1},a_{2})
such that
|a_{1}|\wedge|a_{2}|>r-\bar{\epsilon}
and
|a_{1}|\neq L,|a_{2}|\neq L
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta;A)}{\delta^{\alpha}\Big(\frac{H(1/\eta)}{\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}}=\mu\big(E(\epsilon)\cap h^{-1}(A)\big)

where
\mu
is the measure defined in
eq.

G.65
, and
p(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot;A)
is the conditional probability defined in
eq.

G.67
.

Proof.

Let us start by fixing some notations. Let
T_{1}=0
, and define stopping times
T_{j}=\min\{n>T_{j-1}:\eta|V_{n}|>\delta\}
and inter-arrival times
T^{\prime}_{j}=T_{j}-T_{j-1}
for any
j\geq 1
, and large jump
W_{j}=V_{T_{j}}
for any
j\geq 0
. Note that: first of all, the pair
(T^{\prime}_{i},W_{i})
is independent of
(T^{\prime}_{j},W_{j})
whenever
i\neq j
; besides,
W_{j}
and
T^{\prime}_{j}
are independent for all
j\geq 1
.

Define the following sequence (of random elements)
\textbf{w}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}})
and
\textbf{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l^{*}})
by

\displaystyle w_{j}=\eta W_{j},\ \ t_{j}=\eta T_{j}.

If
V_{0}
has
(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)
-overflow, then the following two events must occur:

•

T^{\prime}_{j}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta
for any
j=2,\cdots,l^{*}
;

•

\eta|W_{j}|>\bar{\delta}
for any
j=1,2,\cdots,l^{*}
;

•

(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\in E(\epsilon)

Therefore, for sufficiently small
\eta
, we now have

\displaystyle p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)\Big)^{l^{*}-1}\cdot\int\mathbbm{1}\Big\{(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\in E(\epsilon)\Big\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\mathbb{P}(\eta W_{1}=dw_{1}|\eta|W_{1}|>\delta)\cdots\mathbb{P}(\eta W_{l^{*}}=dw_{l^{*}}|\ \eta|W_{l^{*}}|>\delta)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\mathbb{P}(\eta T^{\prime}_{2}=dt^{\prime}_{2}|\eta T^{\prime}_{2}\leq 2\bar{t})\cdots\mathbb{P}(\eta T^{\prime}_{l^{*}}=dt^{\prime}_{l^{*}}|\eta T^{\prime}_{l^{*}}\leq 2\bar{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)\Big)^{l^{*}-1}\cdot\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta}\big(E(\epsilon)\cap h^{-1}(A)\big)

(G.68)

where
\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta}
is the Borel-measurable probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{l^{*}}\times\Big(\mathbb{R}_{+}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}
induced by a sequence of independent random variables
(W^{\uparrow}_{1}(\eta,\delta),\cdots,W^{\uparrow}_{l^{*}}(\eta,\delta),T^{\uparrow}_{2}(\eta,\delta),\cdots,T^{\uparrow}_{l^{*}}(\eta,\delta))
such that

•

For any
i=1,\cdots,l^{*}
, the distribution of
W^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta,\delta)
follows from
\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta W_{1}\in\cdot\ \Big|\ \eta|W_{l^{*}}|>\delta\Big)
;

•

For any
i=2,\cdots,l^{*}
, the distribution of
T^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta,\delta)
follows from
\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta T_{1}\in\cdot\ \Big|\ \eta T_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}\Big)
;

•

\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}\Big((\eta W^{\uparrow}_{1}(\eta,\delta),\cdots,\eta W^{\uparrow}_{l^{*}}(\eta,\delta),\eta T^{\uparrow}_{2}(\eta,\delta),\cdots,\eta\sum_{j=2}^{l^{*}}T^{\uparrow}_{j}(\eta,\delta))\in\cdot\Big)
.

Now we study the weak convergence of
W^{\uparrow}_{1},T^{\uparrow}_{1}
:

•

Due to the regularly varying nature of distribution of
Z_{1}
(hence for
W_{1}
), we know that: for any
x>\delta
,

\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta W_{1}>x\ \Big|\ \eta|W_{l^{*}}|>\delta\Big)=p_{+}\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}},\ \ \lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta W_{1}<-x\ \Big|\ \eta|W_{l^{*}}|>\delta\Big)=p_{-}\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha}};

therefore,
W^{\uparrow}_{1}(\eta,\delta)
weakly converges to a (randomly signed) Pareto RV that admits the density

\nu_{\alpha,\delta}(dx)=\mathbbm{1}\{x>0\}p_{+}\frac{\alpha\delta^{\alpha}}{x^{\alpha+1}}+\mathbbm{1}\{x<0\}p_{-}\frac{\alpha\delta^{\alpha}}{|x|^{\alpha+1}}

as
\eta\downarrow 0
;

•

For any
x\in[0,2\bar{t}]
, since
\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor H(\delta/\eta)=0
, it is easy to show that

\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{1-(1-H(\delta/\eta))^{\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor}}{\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor H(\delta/\eta)}=1;

therefore, we have (for any
x\in(0,2\bar{t}]
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\eta T_{1}\leq x\ |\ \eta T_{1}\leq 2\bar{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-(1-H(\delta/\eta))^{\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor}}{1-(1-H(\delta/\eta))^{\lfloor 2\bar{t}/\eta\rfloor}}\rightarrow\frac{x}{2\bar{t}}

as
\eta\downarrow 0
, which implies that
T^{\uparrow}_{1}
converges weakly to a uniform RV on
[0,2\bar{t}]
.

Let us denote the weak limit of measure
\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta}
as
\mu_{\delta,2\bar{t}}
. In the discussion before the Lemma we have shown that, for any
(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\in E(\epsilon)
(with
\delta\in(0,\bar{\delta})
), we have
|w_{i}|\geq\bar{\delta}
and
|t^{\prime}_{i}|\leq 2\bar{t}
; since we require
\delta<\bar{\delta}
, by definition of measures
\mu
and
\mu_{\delta,2\bar{t}}
we have

\mu_{\delta,2\bar{t}}\big(E(\epsilon)\cap h^{-1}(A)\big)=\frac{\delta^{\alpha l^{*}}}{(2\bar{t})^{l^{*}-1}}\cdot\mu\big(E(\epsilon)\cap h^{-1}(A)\big).

For simplicity of notations, we let
E(\epsilon,A)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}E(\epsilon)\cap h^{-1}(A)
. By definition of the set
E(\epsilon)
, we have (recall that
A
is an open interval
(a_{1},a_{2})
that does not overlap with
[s_{-}+\bar{\epsilon},s_{+}-\bar{\epsilon}]
)

\displaystyle E(\epsilon,A)

\displaystyle=h^{-1}\big((-\infty,s_{-}-\epsilon)\cup(s_{+}+\epsilon,\infty)\big)\cap h^{-1}\big((a_{1},a_{2})\big)

\displaystyle=h^{-1}\Big(\big((-\infty,s_{-}-\epsilon)\cup(s_{+}+\epsilon,\infty)\big)\cap(a_{1},a_{2})\Big)

\displaystyle=h^{-1}\big(F(\epsilon,a_{1},a_{2})\big)

where
F(\epsilon,a_{1},a_{2})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\big((-\infty,s_{-}-\epsilon)\cup(s_{+}+\epsilon,\infty)\big)\cap(a_{1},a_{2}).
Meanwhile, it is easy to see that
h
is a continuous mapping, hence

\displaystyle(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\in\partial E(\epsilon,A)\Rightarrow h(\textbf{w},\textbf{t})\in\{s_{-}+\epsilon,s_{+}-\epsilon,a_{1},a_{2}\}.

Fix some
s
with
s\neq\pm L,|s|>(l^{*}-1)b+\bar{\epsilon}
. For any fixed real numbers
t_{2},\cdots,t_{l^{*}-1}
,
w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}}
, if
h(w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}},t_{2},\cdots,t_{l^{*}-1},t)=s
, then since
\widetilde{\textbf{x}}(t_{l^{*}}-1,0;w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}},t_{2},\cdots,t_{l^{*}-1},t)\in[s-b,s+b]
, due to Assumption 1 (in particular, there is no point
x
on this interval with
|f^{\prime}(x)|\leq c_{0}
), there exists at most one possible
t
that makes
h(w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}},t_{2},\cdots,t_{l^{*}-1},t)=s
. Therefore, let
W^{*}_{j}
be iid RVs from law
\nu_{\alpha,\delta}
defined above, and
(T^{*,\prime}_{j})_{j\geq 2}
be iid RVs from Unif
[0,\bar{2t}]
,
T^{*}_{0}=0,T^{*}_{k}=\sum_{j=2}^{k}T^{*,\prime}_{j}
. By conditioning on all
W^{*}_{j}
and all
T^{*,\prime}_{2},\cdots,T^{*,\prime}_{l^{*}-1}
, we must have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(h(W^{*}_{1},\cdots,W^{*}_{j},T^{*}_{2},\cdots,T^{*}_{l^{*}})=s\ \Big|\ W^{*}_{1}=dw_{1},\cdots,W^{*}_{l^{*}}=dw_{l^{*}},

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ T^{*,\prime}_{2}=dt_{2},\cdots,T^{*,\prime}_{l^{*}-1}=dt_{l^{*}-1}\Big)=0

(G.69)

which implies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(h(W^{*}_{1},\cdots,W^{*}_{j},T^{*}_{2},\cdots,T^{*}_{l^{*}})=s\Big)=0

hence

\displaystyle\mu\Big(\partial E(\epsilon,A)\Big)=0.

By Portmanteau theorem (see Theorem 2.1 of
Billingsley 2013
) we have

\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta}(E(\epsilon,A))=\mu_{\delta,2\bar{t}}(E(\epsilon,A)).

Collecting the results we have and using
eq.

G.68
, we can see that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta;A)}{\Big(\frac{H(1/\eta)}{\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}\delta^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{(2\bar{t})^{l^{*}-1}\cdot p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta;A)}{\delta^{\alpha l^{*}}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)\Big)^{l^{*}-1}}\cdot\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{(2\bar{t})^{l^{*}-1}\cdot p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta;A)}{\delta^{\alpha l^{*}}\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)\Big)^{l^{*}-1}}\cdot\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mu(E(\epsilon,A))\cdot\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}.

Fix some
\kappa>1
. From Lemma
G.4
and the regularly varying nature of function
H
, we get

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa^{l^{*}-1}\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{2\bar{t}H(\delta/\eta)/\eta}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}=\kappa^{l^{*}-1}.

Due to the arbitrariness of
\kappa>1
, we have established that

\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta;A)}{\Big(\frac{H(1/\eta)}{\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}\delta^{\alpha}}\leq\mu(E(\epsilon)).

The lower bound can be shown by an argument symmetric to the one for upper bound.
∎

The following result is an immediate corollary of Lemma
G.15
.

Corollary G.16
.

For any
\epsilon\in(-\bar{\epsilon},\bar{\epsilon}),\delta\in(0,b\wedge\bar{\delta})
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{p(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)}{\delta^{\alpha}\Big(\frac{H(1/\eta)}{\eta}\Big)^{l^{*}-1}}=\mu\big(E(\epsilon)\big)

where
\mu
is the measure defined in
eq.

G.65
, and
p(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
is the conditional probability defined in
eq.

G.66
.

Define the following stopping times:

\displaystyle\sigma(\eta)

\displaystyle=\min\{n\geq 0:X^{\eta}_{n}\notin\Omega\};

(G.70)

\displaystyle R(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\min\{n\geq T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta):X^{\eta}_{n}\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]\}.

(G.71)

\sigma
indicate the time that the iterates escape the current attraction field, while
R
denotes the time the SGD iterates return to a small neighborhood of the local minimum after first exit from this small neighborhood. In the next few results, we study the probability of several atypical scenarios when SGD iterates make attempts to escape
\Omega
or return to local minimum after the attempt fails. First, we show that, when starting from the local minimum, it is very unlikely to escape with less than
l^{*}
big jumps.

Lemma G.17
.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon}),N>0
, the following claim holds for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(\sigma(\eta)<R(\epsilon,\eta),\ \sigma(\eta)<T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

as
\eta\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

Based on the given
\epsilon>0
, fix some
\widetilde{\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon}{4\exp(2M\hat{t}(\epsilon))}
.
Recall the definition of
\hat{t}(\epsilon)
in
eq.

G.38
.

First, using Lemma
G.14
, we know that for sufficiently small
\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}\Big(A^{\times}_{1}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

(G.72)

where

\displaystyle A^{\times}_{1}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)=\Big\{\exists n<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\ s.t.\ |X^{\eta}_{n}(x)|>3\epsilon\Big\}.

Define event

A^{\times}_{2}(\widetilde{\epsilon},\delta,\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\Big\{\exists j=2,\cdots,l^{*}\ s.t.\ \max_{k=1,2,\cdots,T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)-T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)-1}\eta|Z_{T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)+1}^{\leq\delta,\eta}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta,\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)+k}|>\widetilde{\epsilon}\Big\}.

From Lemma
G.12
, we know that for sufficiently small
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(A^{\times}_{2}(\widetilde{\epsilon},\delta,\eta)\Big)=o(\eta^{N}).

(G.73)

From now on, we only consider such
\delta
that
eq.

G.72
eq.

G.73
hold.

On event
\Big(A^{\times}_{1}\cup A^{\times}_{2}\Big)^{c}\cap\{\sigma(\eta)<R(\epsilon,\eta)\}\cap\{\sigma(\eta)>T_{1}^{\eta}(\delta)\}
, we must have
\sigma(\eta)>T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)
and

\displaystyle T^{\eta}_{2}(\delta)\wedge\sigma(\eta)-T_{1}^{\eta}(\delta)<2\hat{t}(\epsilon)/\eta.

Otherwise, due to Lemma
G.6
and
G.9
, we know that at step
\widetilde{t}=T_{1}^{\eta}(\delta)+\lfloor\hat{t}(\epsilon)/\eta\rfloor
, we have

\displaystyle|X^{\eta}_{\widetilde{t}}|<2\epsilon,\text{ and }|X^{\eta}_{n}|\leq\bar{\epsilon}\ \ \forall n\leq\widetilde{t}

for any sufficiently small
\eta
.
By repeating this argument inductively, we obtain the following result: define

\displaystyle J=\min\{j=1,2,\cdots:\sigma(\eta)\in[T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta),T^{\eta}_{j+1}(\delta))\},

then on event
\Big(A^{\times}_{1}\cup A^{\times}_{2}\Big)^{c}\cap\{\sigma(\eta)<R(\epsilon,\eta),\ \sigma(\eta)<T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)\}
, we must have

\displaystyle T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)\wedge\sigma(\eta)-T_{j-1}^{\eta}(\delta)\wedge\sigma(\eta)<2\hat{t}(\epsilon)/\eta\ \ \forall j=2,3,\cdots,J.

(G.74)

Furthermore, using this bound and Lemma
G.9
, we know that on event
\Big(A^{\times}_{1}\cup A^{\times}_{2}\Big)^{c}\cap\{\sigma(\eta)<R(\epsilon,\eta),\ \sigma(\eta)<T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)\}
,

•

|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)}|\leq|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)}|+b+\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}
for all
j=2,3,J-1
,

•

|X^{\eta}_{\sigma(\eta)}|\leq|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{J-1}(\delta)}|+\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}

However, this implies

\displaystyle|X^{\eta}_{\sigma(\eta)}|\leq l^{*}(\bar{\epsilon}+\epsilon)+(l^{*}-1)b<r

and contradicts the definition of
\sigma(\eta)
. In summary,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(\sigma(\eta)<R(\epsilon,\eta),\ \sigma(\eta)<T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(A^{\times}_{1}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\cup A^{\times}_{2}(\widetilde{\epsilon},\delta,\eta)\Big)=o(\eta^{N}).

∎

The following two results follow immediately from the proof above, especially the inductive argument leading to bound
eq.

G.74
, and we state them without repeating the deatils of the proof.

Corollary G.18
.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon}),N>0
, the following claim holds for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)\leq\sigma(\eta)\wedge R(\epsilon,\eta),\ \text{and }\exists j=2,3,\cdots,l^{*}\ s.t.\ T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)-T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)>2\hat{t}(\epsilon)/\eta\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\eta^{N})\ \ \ \ \ \text{as }\eta\downarrow 0.

Corollary G.19
.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon}),N>0
, the following claim holds for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(R(\epsilon,\eta)<T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)\wedge\sigma(\eta),\ R(\epsilon,\eta)-T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>2l^{*}\hat{t}(\epsilon)/\eta\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

as
\eta\downarrow 0
.

In the next result, we show that, if the inter-arrival time between some large jumps are too long, or some large jumps are still not
large enough
, then it is very unlikely that the SGD iterates could escape at the time of
l^{*}-
th large jump (or even get close enough to the boundary of the attraction field).

Lemma G.20
.

Given
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon})
and any
N>0
, the following holds for all
\delta>0
that are sufficiently small:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}_{x}(B^{\times}_{2}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta))=o(\eta^{N}).

where

\displaystyle B^{\times}_{2}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\{T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}(\delta)\leq\sigma(\eta)\wedge R(\epsilon,\eta)\}\cap\Big\{\exists j=2,3,\cdots,l^{*}\ s.t.\ T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)-T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)>\bar{t}/\eta

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{or }\exists j=1,2,\cdots,l^{*}\ s.t.\ \eta|W^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)|\leq\bar{\delta}\Big\}\cap\{|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}}|\geq r-\bar{\epsilon}\}.

Proof.

Let
A^{\times}_{1},A^{\times}_{2}
be the events defined in the proof of Lemma
G.17
. Based on the given
\epsilon>0
, fix some
\widetilde{\epsilon}=\frac{\epsilon}{4\exp(2M\hat{t}(\epsilon))}
.

Let
J=\min\{j=2,3,\cdots:T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)-T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)>\bar{t}/\eta\}
. On event
\Big(A^{\times}_{1}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\cup A^{\times}_{2}(\widetilde{\epsilon},\delta,\eta)\Big)^{c}\cap\{J\leq l^{*}\}
, from Lemma
G.6
and
G.9
and the definition of constant
\bar{t}
, we know that

•

|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)}|\leq|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)}|+b+\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}
for all
j=2,3,J-1
;

•

|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{J}(\delta)}|\leq 2\bar{\epsilon}

•

|X^{\eta}_{n}|<s-\bar{\epsilon}\ \ \forall n\leq T^{\eta}_{J}(\delta)

Now starting from step
T^{\eta}_{J}(\delta)
, by using Lemma
G.6
and
G.9
again one can see that

•

|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)}|\leq|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)}|+b+\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}
for all
j=J+1,\cdots,l^{*}
.

Combining these results, we have that
|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}}|<r-\bar{\epsilon}
on event
\Big(A^{\times}_{1}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\cup A^{\times}_{2}(\widetilde{\epsilon},\delta,\eta)\Big)^{c}\cap\{J\leq l^{*}\}
.

Next, define
J^{\prime}=\min\{j=1,2,\cdots;\eta|W^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)|\leq\bar{\delta}\}
. Similarly, on event
\Big(A^{\times}_{1}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\cup A^{\times}_{2}(\widetilde{\epsilon},\delta,\eta)\Big)^{c}\cap\{J>l^{*}\}\cap\{J^{\prime}\leq l^{*}\}
, using Lemma
G.6
and
G.9
again one can see that

•

|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)}|\leq|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)}|+b+\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}
for all
j=1,2,\cdots,l^{*},j\neq J^{\prime}
;

•

|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{J}(\delta)}|\leq|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{J-1}(\delta)}|+\bar{\delta}+\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}
for all
j=1,2,\cdots,l^{*},j\neq J^{\prime}
.

Since
\bar{\delta}\in(0,\bar{\epsilon})
, we have
|X^{\eta}_{T^{\eta}_{l^{*}}}|<r-\bar{\epsilon}
on this event.

In summary, the following bound

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[-2\epsilon,2\epsilon]}\mathbb{P}_{x}(B^{\times}_{2})\leq\mathbb{P}(A^{\times}_{1}(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\cup A^{\times}_{2}(\widetilde{\epsilon},\delta,\eta))=o(\eta^{N})

holds for any
\delta
that is sufficiently small, which is established in Lemma
G.17
. This conclude the proof.
∎

In the next lemma, we show that, starting from the local minimum, it is unlikely that the SGD iterates will be right at the boundary of the attraction field after
l^{*}
large jumps. Recall that there are
n_{\text{min}}
attraction fields on
f
, and excluding
s_{0}=-\infty,s_{n_{\text{min}}}=\infty
the remaining points
s_{1},\cdots,s_{n_{\text{min}}-1}
are the boundaries of the attraction fields.

Lemma G.21
.

There exists a function
\Psi(\cdot):\mathbb{R}^{+}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{+}
satisfying
\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\Psi(\epsilon)=0
such that the following claim folds. Given
\epsilon\in\big(0,\bar{\epsilon}/(3\bar{\rho}+3\widetilde{\rho}+9)\big)
, we have
