As stated above,
Theorem
I.1
deals with general case where the noise distribution is allowed to have different heavy-tailed indices along different directions (see Assumption
I.6
).
As a special case, it is worth noticing that if a single heavy-tailed index
\alpha
can be used to describe the tail behavior of noises along any direction (this can be easily guaranteed if the heavy-tailed noise is manually injected into a light-tailed setting),
then the minimum cost
J_{\mathcal{G}}
will be equal to
l^{*}(\alpha-1)
where
l^{*}
is the minimum number of jumps required for escape.
The readers can see that this is a natural extension of our
\mathbb{R}^{1}
results,
implying that the same strong preference for “wide” minima still hold in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
under truncated heavy-tailed SGD.
To be specific, we work with the following assumption about the noise distribution.

Assumption I.10
.

\mathbb{E}Z_{1}=\textbf{0}
.
Besides,
there exists some
\alpha>1
,
a slowly-varying functions
l
,
a probability measures
S
on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with support
\text{supp}(S_{j})=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that

•

For the measure
\nu(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}\in\cdot\cap E_{j})
, we have

\displaystyle t^{\alpha}\cdot l(t)\cdot\nu\circ\textbf{T}^{-1}\big(t\cdot dr\times d\theta\big)\rightarrow\nu_{\alpha}(dr)\times S(d\theta)\ \ \ \text{as }t\rightarrow\infty

(I.11)

in the sense of
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax\mathbb{O})
. Here
\nu_{\alpha}
is a Borel measure defined on
(0,\infty)
satisfying
\nu_{\alpha}[t,\infty)=t^{-\alpha}\ \ \forall t>0
;

•

The measure
S
is absolutely continuous w.r.t. spherical measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Compared to Assumption
I.6
,
one can easily see that Assumption
I.10
is a stronger version with the specific proviso that a single index
\alpha
can describe the tail of the noise distribution along any direction in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The first exit time results now admit a simplified form.
In particular, the cost of jumps will degenerate to the
count
of jumps in the sense that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}J(w_{i})=k(\alpha-1)

for any
k\geq 1
and any
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax\mathbb{O})^{k}
.
Moreover, we now have
J_{\mathcal{G}}=l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\cdot(\alpha-1)
where

\displaystyle l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{k\in\mathbb{N}:\ \exists\bm{t}^{(k)}\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}_{+},\bm{w}^{(k)}\in\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax\mathbb{O}\big)^{k}\text{ s.t. }\widetilde{\bm{x}}(\sum_{j=1}^{k}t_{k},\bm{0};\bm{t}^{(k)},\bm{w}^{(k)})\notin\mathcal{G}\}

and the measure
\mu
can now be expressed as

\mu=(\prod_{i=1}^{l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}\nu_{\alpha})\times(\prod_{i=1}^{l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}S)\times\bm{m}_{\text{Leb}}^{l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}-1}
.
Therefore, the following theorem is merely a restatement of Theorem
I.1
in this simplified setting.
Still, we present the result to highlight the role of the minimum jump number
J_{\mathcal{G}}
in the first exit time.

Theorem I.2
.

Let Assumptions
I.1
-
I.5
,
I.10
and
I.7
-
I.9
hold.
There exists a function
\lambda(\eta)
that is regularly varying (as
\eta\downarrow 0
) with index
1+l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}(\alpha-1)
such that,
for any
x\in\mathcal{G}
and any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\sigma(\eta)\lambda(\eta)>t)=\exp(-qt)

where the constant
q=\mu\big(h^{-1}(\mathcal{G}^{c})\big)
.

It is clear that first exit time since the first exit time is roughly of order
1/\eta^{1+(\alpha-1)l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}}
. Therefore, even in the general
\mathbb{R}^{d}
case, the quantity
l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}
, i.e. the minimum count of jumps to escape from a domain
\mathcal{G}
, induces a hierarchy of first exit time as the first exit time from the domain with largest
l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}
(requiring most number of jumps to escape) will dominate the first exit time from other regions.

Lastly, if we work under the standard setting such as the ones in
Zhou et al. 2020
where local strong convexity of
f
in the domain
\mathcal{G}
is assumed,
the results can be further simplified and the minimum jump number
l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}
will be directly tied to the
width
of the each domain.
Specifically,
the quantity
r_{\mathcal{G}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\sup_{y\in\mathcal{G}}\left\lVert y\right\rVert
can be interpreted the effective width or radius of
\mathcal{G}
,
and we work with the following assumption.

Assumption I.11
.

The function
f
is strongly convex on the closed ball
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert y\right\rVert\leq r_{\mathcal{G}}\}
.

Two consequences follow immediately from this assumption.
First of all, there exists some constant
c>0
such that

\left\lVert\bm{x}_{t}(x)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert x\right\rVert e^{-ct}\ \ \forall x\in\{y:\ \left\lVert y\right\rVert<r_{\mathcal{G}}\}.

As a result, we must have
l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\geq r_{\mathcal{G}}/b.

Next, as long as
r_{\mathcal{G}}/b
is not an integer (which holds for Lebesgue almost every
b>0
), for
k=\lceil r_{\mathcal{G}}/b\rceil
we can find
t_{2}>0,\cdots,t_{k}>0
and
w_{1}\neq\bm{0},\cdots,w_{k}\neq\bm{0}
where
\widetilde{\bm{x}}\big(s,\bm{0};(0,t_{2},\cdots,t_{k}),(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})\big)\notin\mathcal{G}
for some
s>0
.
In fact, it is worth noticing that Assumption
I.8
now degenerates to the condition that
r_{\mathcal{G}}/b
is not an integer,
and we now know that for any such
b>0
, we have
l^{*}_{\mathcal{G}}\leq\lceil r_{\mathcal{G}}/b\rceil.

In summary, we have established the following result indicating that the strong preference for wider minima under truncated heavy-tailed SGD still persists in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
given proper convexity assumption on
f
.

Theorem I.3
.

Let Assumptions
I.1
-
I.5
,
I.10
-
I.11
and
I.7
-
I.9
hold.
There exists a function
\lambda(\eta)
that is regularly varying (as
\eta\downarrow 0
) with index
1+l^{*}(\alpha-1)
such that,
for any
x\in\mathcal{G}
and any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\sigma(\eta)\lambda(\eta)>t)=\exp(-qt)

where the constant
q=\mu\big(h^{-1}(\mathcal{G}^{c})\big)
and
l^{*}=\lceil r_{\mathcal{G}}/b\rceil
.

I.3
Relaxing the Technical Assumptions

In order to achieve the tightest characterization of the first exit time, some assumptions introduced above are slightly stronger than the ones in
Imkeller et al. 2010a
.
For instance, in Assumption
I.3
we require
\partial\mathcal{G}
to be of class
C^{2}
while the assumption (A3) in
Imkeller et al. 2010a
only requires it to be a
C^{1}
manifold.
Besides, Assumption
I.6
requires that
S_{j}
, the limiting distribution of the directions of each heavy-tailed component in noises, to be absolutely continuous w.r.t. the spherical measure.
As will be stressed in Section
K.1
, these conditions are only imposed to prove Lemma
K.2
,
thus ensuring that Assumption
I.9
holds for almost every
b>0
.
Briefly speaking, all the efforts to guarantee Assumption
I.9
allows us to conclude that the law of scaled first exit time
\lambda(\eta)\sigma(\eta)
converges exactly to that of
Exp(q)
with
q=\mu\big(h^{-1}(\mathcal{G}^{c})\big)
.

Fortunately, the discussion below will show that, even when we relax all the extra technical assumptions above (hence removing Assumption
I.9
),
the order of the first exit time is still dictated by the minimum cost for escape
J_{\mathcal{G}}
,
and a similar result about the first exit time can be obtained
where the distribution of the scaled first exit time
\lambda(\eta)\sigma(\eta)
will be asymptotically bounded by two Exponential RVs.

Specifically, we reiterate that the first half of Assumption
I.3
, i.e.
\partial\mathcal{G}
is a differential manifold, will only be applied to prove Lemma
K.2
.
The second half of the proof, namely the lower bound in
eq.

I.1
,
only serves the ensure that for any
x\in\mathcal{G}
, we have

\bm{x}_{t}(x)\in\mathcal{G}\ \ \forall t\geq 0

so that the gradient flow starting in
\mathcal{G}
will never leave this domain.
Now let us focus on the open sets
\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}
for some small
\epsilon>0
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\ \bm{d}(y,\mathcal{G})<\epsilon\},\ \ \ \mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{y\in\mathcal{G}:\ \bm{d}(y,\mathcal{G}^{c})>\epsilon\}.

Note that
\cap_{\epsilon}\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon}=\overline{\mathcal{G}},\ \cup_{\epsilon}\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}=\mathcal{G}
and for any positive reals
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}
such that
\epsilon_{1}\neq\epsilon_{2}
and the two reals are small enough for
G_{\epsilon_{1}}
and
G_{\epsilon_{2}}
to be non-empty,

\partial\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon_{1}}\cap\partial\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon_{2}}=\emptyset,\ \ \ \ \partial\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon_{1}}\cap\partial\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon_{2}}=\emptyset.

Given
eq.

I.1
, as well as the smoothness of the vector field
\nabla f
(see Assumption
I.2
) and the fact that
\mathcal{G}
is connected, bounded and open and contains a unique attractor
\bm{0}
(see Assumption
I.1
and
I.5
),
it is easy to see that for all
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, we will have
\bm{x}_{t}(x)\in\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon}\ \ \forall t\geq 0
for any
x\in\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon}
and
\bm{x}_{t}(x)\in\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}\ \ \forall t\geq 0
for any
x\in\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}
.

Meanwhile, in our proof below we will establish
eq.

J.17

(which, again, does not require the
C^{2}
class assumption on
\partial\mathcal{G}
or the absolute continuity assumption on the measures
S_{j}
).
Note that
eq.

J.17
implies the existence of
\Delta>0
such that
\mu\big(h^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{\Delta})\big)<\infty
.
Therefore, for all but only countably many
\epsilon\in(0,\Delta)
, we have
\mu\big(h^{-1}(\partial\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon})\big)=\mu\big(h^{-1}(\partial\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon})\big)=0
.
In other words, we can find a sequence of
\epsilon_{n}
with
\lim_{n}\epsilon_{n}=0
such that

\mu\big(h^{-1}(\partial\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon_{n}})\big)=\mu\big(h^{-1}(\partial\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon_{n}})\big)=0\ \ \forall n\geq 1

.
Also, for a fixed
b>0
that ensures Assumption
I.7
and
I.8
, one can see that similar conditions will also hold for sets
\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon}
and
\mathbb{G}_{\epsilon}
as long as
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small.
In summary, if we consider first exit times

\displaystyle\sigma^{n}(\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\min\{k\geq 0:\ X^{\eta}_{k}\notin\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon_{n}}\},\ \ \ \sigma_{n}(\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\min\{k\geq 0:\ X^{\eta}_{k}\notin\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon_{n}}\}

with the obvious bounds that
\sigma_{n}(\eta)\leq\sigma(\eta)\leq\sigma^{n}(\eta)
for all
n\geq 1
,
then it suffices to apply Theorem
I.1
directly onto
\sigma_{n}(\eta),\sigma^{n}(\eta)
.
Note that we did not attempt to establish that
\partial\mathcal{G}^{\epsilon}
or
\partial\mathcal{G}_{\epsilon}
are differential manifolds.
However, we reiterate that this is not needed since the
C^{2}
manifold condition in Assumption
I.3
only serves to ensure that Assumption
I.9
holds for almost surely every
b>0
, which we sidestep by picking a proper sequence
\epsilon_{n}
to explicitly satisfy the condition.
More formally speaking, consider the following relax ted assumptions.

Assumption I.12
.

The boundary set
\partial\mathcal{G}
is a
(n-1)
-dimensional manifold of class
C^{1}
such that the vector field
n(\cdot)
of the outer normals on
\partial\mathcal{G}
exists with

\displaystyle\nabla f(v)^{T}n(v)\geq c_{0}\ \ \ \forall v\in\partial\mathcal{G}

for some constant
c_{0}>0
.

Assumption I.13
.

\mathbb{E}Z_{1}=\textbf{0}
.
Besides, there exist a positive integer
m\geq 1
, a sequence
0<\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\cdots<\alpha_{m}<\infty
, a sequence of slowly-varying functions
(l_{1},\cdots,l_{m})
, a sequence of probability measures
(S_{1},\cdots,S_{m})
on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
with support
F_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\text{supp}(S_{j})
as closed sets on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that

•

F_{i}\cap F_{j}=\emptyset
for any
i\neq j
;

•

For any
j\in[m]
, define the cone
E_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\textbf{T}_{\theta}^{-1}(F_{j})\cup\mathbb{O}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and measure
\mathbb{P}^{(j)}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}(Z_{1}\in\cdot\cap E_{j})
, we have

\displaystyle t^{\alpha_{j}}\cdot l_{j}(t)\cdot\mathbb{P}^{(j)}\circ\textbf{T}^{-1}\big(t\cdot dr\times d\theta\big)\rightarrow\nu_{\alpha_{j}}(dr)\times S_{j}(d\theta)\ \ \ \text{as }t\rightarrow\infty

in the sense of
\mathbb{M}(E_{j}\char 92\relax\mathbb{O})
. Here
\nu_{\alpha}
is a Borel measure defined on
(0,\infty)
satisfying
\nu_{\alpha}[t,\infty)=t^{-\alpha}\ \ \forall t>0
;

•

For measure
\mathbb{P}^{(0)}(\cdot)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\mathbb{P}\big(Z_{1}\in\cdot\ \char 92\relax(\cup_{j=1}^{m}E_{j})\big)
and any
\alpha>0
,

\displaystyle t^{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{P}^{(0)}\circ\textbf{T}^{-1}\big(t\cdot dr\times d\theta\big)\rightarrow 0\ \ \ \text{as }t\rightarrow\infty

in the sense of
\mathbb{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax\mathbb{O})
.

We say Assumptions
I.1
-
I.2
,
I.12
,
I.4
-
I.5
,
I.13
,
I.7
-
I.8
are the set of
relaxed assumptions
.
The discussion above implies that the following result is an immediate consequence from Theorem
I.1
.

Theorem I.4
.

Let the relaxed assumptions hold.
There exists a function
\lambda(\eta)
that is regularly varying (as
\eta\downarrow 0
) with index
(1+J_{\mathcal{G}})
such that,
for any
x\in\mathcal{G}
and any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\exp(-qt)\leq\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\sigma(\eta)\lambda(\eta)>t)\leq\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\sigma(\eta)\lambda(\eta)>t)\leq\exp(-q^{\uparrow}t)

with
q=\mu\big(h^{-1}(\mathcal{G}^{c})\big),q^{\uparrow}=\mu\Big(h^{-1}\big((\overline{\mathcal{G}})^{c}\big)\Big)
.

Appendix J
Proof of Theorem
I.1

This section is devoted to establishing Theorem
I.1
.
For clarity of the exposition, we break down the proof into several major steps, each contained in a subsection below.

J.1
Picking constants
\bar{t},\bar{\epsilon},\bar{\delta}

The goal of this subsection is to fix several important constants that characterize the typical sizes and inter-arrival times of large perturbations in SGD trajectory that can cause the escape from domain
\mathcal{G}
.
We will abuse the notations slightly when referencing certain constants, and quantities such as
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}
may not be equal to the ones used in assumptions above.

First, from Assumption
I.6
and the definition of
J_{\mathcal{G}}
, we can define

\displaystyle l^{*}=\lceil J_{\mathcal{G}}/(\alpha_{1}-1)\rceil+1\ \

(J.1)

and we must have
\infty>l^{*}>k^{*}
. Meanwhile, from Assumption
I.8
, we can find
\bar{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle\bm{d}\big(\mathcal{G}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}),\mathcal{G}^{c})>100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon}

(J.2)

for any
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
with
\sum_{j=1}^{m}i_{j}(\alpha_{j}-1)<J_{\mathcal{G}}
, as well as

\displaystyle\exists x\in\mathcal{G}(\bm{i}^{*})\text{ such that }\bm{d}(x,\mathcal{G})>100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon}.

Furthermore, Assumption
I.5
implies that
\nabla f(v)\neq\textbf{0}
for any
v\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}\char 92\relax\{\textbf{0}\}
. From Assumptions
I.3
,
I.4
and
I.5
(and by picking a smaller
\bar{\epsilon}>0
if needed), one can see the existence of some
c_{0}>0,\bar{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{x}_{t}(x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq e^{-c_{0}t}\left\lVert x\right\rVert\ \ \forall t\geq 0,\ x\in\overline{B(\textbf{0},\bar{\epsilon})},

(J.3)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla f(x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geq c_{0}\ \ \forall x\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}\char 92\relax B(\textbf{0},\bar{\epsilon}),

(J.4)

\displaystyle\bm{d}\big(\bm{x}_{t}(x),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)

\displaystyle\geq\bm{d}(x,\mathcal{G}^{c})+c_{0}t\ \ \ \text{if}\ \bm{x}_{s}(x)\in\mathcal{G}\text{ and }\bm{d}\big(\bm{x}_{s}(x),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)\leq\bar{\epsilon}\ \forall s\in[0,t].

(J.5)

Here
B(x,r)=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert<r\}
is the open ball centered at
x
with radius
r>0
. One immediate consequence from
eq.

J.5
is that

\displaystyle\bm{d}\big(\bm{x}_{t}(x),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)\geq\min\{\bm{d}(x,\mathcal{G}^{c}),\bar{\epsilon}\}\ \ \ \forall x\in\mathcal{G},t\geq 0.

(J.6)

Besides, the boundedness of domain
\mathcal{G}
and smoothness of
f(\cdot)
imply the existence of some
M>0
such that

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{G}}

\displaystyle\subseteq B(\textbf{0},M),

(J.7)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla f(x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq M\ \ \forall x\in\overline{\mathcal{G}},

(J.8)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f(x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq M\ \ \forall x\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}

(J.9)

where for the matrix norm we use spectral norm.

Recall the definitions of the configuration sets
\mathcal{A}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})
, the unique configuration
\bm{i}^{*}
and count of jumps
k^{*}
to trigger the exit with minimum cost (see Assumption
I.7
and the remark underneath). The following three results provide control on the sizes and inter-arrival times of typical jumps that can trigger the escape from
\mathcal{G}
. The proofs involve analyses of the deterministic dynamical system
\widetilde{\bm{x}}_{t}
and will be provided in Section
K
.
In particular, we introduce another concept as
type
of jumps
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})
that is similar configuration
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})
and configuration sets
\mathcal{A}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax\mathbb{O})^{k}
,
we say that the
(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})
is of type-
\bm{j}
for some
\bm{j}=(j_{1},\cdots,j_{k})\in\{1,2,\cdots,m\}^{k}
iff

\displaystyle w_{i}\in E_{j_{i}}\ \forall i=1,\cdots,k.

(J.10)

In other words, if
\bm{w}^{1}
and
\bm{w}^{2}
are of the same type
\bm{j}
, then they have the same cardinality
|\bm{w}^{1}|=|\bm{w}^{2}|=|\bm{j}|=k
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
;
moreover, for any
i\in[k]
, the jumps
w^{1}_{i}
and
w^{2}_{i}
are both in the cone
E_{j_{i}}
so both jumps point at directions in cone
E_{j_{i}}
and have the same cost
J(w^{1}_{i})=J(w^{2}_{i})=\alpha_{j_{i}}-1
for some
j_{i}\in[m]
.
Define the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{type}}_{\bm{j}}=\{\bm{w}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax\mathbb{O})^{|\bm{j}|}:\ \bm{w}\text{ is of type-}\bm{j}\}.

The accumulated cost for any
\bm{w}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{type}}_{\bm{j}}
is defined as
\mathcal{J}_{\text{type}}(\bm{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{|\bm{j}|}(\alpha_{j_{i}}-1)
. Lastly, define the skip-one accumulated cost for any type
\bm{j}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\text{type}}^{\downarrow}(\bm{j})=\max\{\sum_{i=1,\cdots,|\bm{j}|,i\neq k}(\alpha_{j_{i}}-1):\ k=1,2,\cdots,|\bm{j}|\},

i.e.,
\mathcal{J}^{\downarrow}_{\text{type}}(\bm{j})
is the highest possible accumulated cost if we remove one element in
\bm{w}
for
\bm{w}\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{type}}_{\bm{j}}
.

Lemma J.1
.

There exist some
\bar{t}\in(0,\infty),\bar{\delta}\in(0,\infty),\epsilon_{0}>0
such that the following claim holds.
Let
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{j}=(j_{1},\cdots,j_{k^{\prime}})\in\{1,2,\cdots,m\}^{k^{\prime}}
be such that
\mathcal{J}^{\downarrow}_{\text{type}}(\bm{j})<J_{\mathcal{G}}
.
For any
\bm{t}^{(k^{\prime})}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{k^{\prime}})\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{\prime}-1}_{+},\bm{w}^{(k^{\prime})}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{type}}_{\bm{j}}
,
the following set of conditions

\displaystyle t_{j}

\displaystyle<\bar{t}\ \ \forall j=2,3,\cdots,k^{\prime}

(J.11)

\displaystyle\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert

\displaystyle>\bar{\delta}\ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,k^{\prime}.

(J.12)

is the necessary condition for
\inf_{s\geq 0}\bm{d}\big(\widetilde{\bm{x}}(s,\bm{0};\ \bm{t}^{(k)},\bm{w}^{(k)}),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)\leq\epsilon_{0}
.

Lemma J.2
.

Let
\bar{t},\bar{\delta},\epsilon_{0}
be the positive constants prescribed in Lemma
J.1
.
There exists some
\epsilon_{1}>0
such that the following claim holds.

Let
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{j}=(j_{1},\cdots,j_{k^{\prime}})\in\{1,2,\cdots,m\}^{k^{\prime}}
be such that
\mathcal{J}_{\text{type}}^{\downarrow}(\bm{j})<J_{\mathcal{G}}
.
For any
x\in B(\bm{0},\epsilon_{1})
,
any
\bm{t}^{(k^{\prime})}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{k^{\prime}})\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{\prime}-1}_{+},\bm{w}^{(k^{\prime})}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{type}}_{\bm{j}}
,
the following set of conditions

\displaystyle t_{j}

\displaystyle<2\bar{t}\ \ \forall j=2,3,\cdots,k^{\prime}

(J.13)

\displaystyle\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert

\displaystyle>\bar{\delta}/2\ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,k^{\prime}.

(J.14)

is the necessary condition for
\inf_{s\geq 0}\bm{d}\big(\widetilde{\bm{x}}(s,x;\ \bm{t}^{(k)},\bm{w}^{(k)}),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)\leq\epsilon_{0}/2
.

Lemma J.3
.

There exist some
\epsilon_{0}>0,\delta_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{s\geq 0}\bm{d}\big(\widetilde{\bm{x}}(s,x;\ \bm{t}^{(k)},\bm{w}^{(k)}),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)>\epsilon_{0}

for all
x\in B(\bm{0},\delta_{0})
, all
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
with
\mathcal{J}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})<J_{\mathcal{G}}
and
k=\sum_{j=1}^{m}i_{j}
, and all
\bm{w}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{A}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}),\ \bm{t}^{(k)}\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}_{+}
.

Similar to the ODE path
\bm{x}
, we defined the following paths with rate
\eta>0
:

\displaystyle\dot{(\bm{x}^{\eta}_{t})}(x)

\displaystyle=-\eta\nabla f\big(\bm{x}^{\eta}_{t}(x)\big)\ \ \forall t\geq 0,

(J.15)

\displaystyle\bm{x}^{\eta}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=x.

(J.16)

In other words, ODE flow
\bm{x}_{t}
is equivalent to
\bm{x}^{\eta}_{t}
with rate
\eta=1
. The next result gives us an upper bound for the return time to a neighborhood of the local minimum as for the gradient flow.

Lemma J.4
.

For
\tau_{ODE}^{\eta}(x,\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{t\geq 0:\ \bm{x}^{\eta}_{t}(x)\in\overline{B(\bm{0},\epsilon)}\}
, there exists
c_{1}\in(0,\infty)
such that for any
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon})
,

\displaystyle\tau_{ODE}^{\eta}(x,\epsilon)\leq\frac{c_{1}+c_{1}\log(1/\epsilon)}{\eta}\ \ \ \forall x\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}.

Proof.

Due to
eq.

J.4
, we know that
T_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\frac{\sup_{x\in\bar{\mathcal{G}}}f(x)-\inf_{x\in\bar{\mathcal{G}}}f(x)}{c_{0}}<\infty
and

\sup_{x\in\bar{\mathcal{G}}}\tau^{1}_{ODE}(x,\bar{\epsilon})\leq T_{0}.

Furthermore, from
eq.

J.3
one can see that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\bar{\mathcal{G}}}\tau^{1}_{ODE}(x,\epsilon)-\tau^{1}_{ODE}(x,\bar{\epsilon})\leq\frac{\log(1/\epsilon)-\log(1/\bar{\epsilon})}{c_{0}}.

Now combining these two bounds with a
1/\eta
time scaling, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\bar{\mathcal{G}}}\tau^{\eta}_{ODE}(x,\epsilon)\leq\frac{c_{1}+c_{1}\log(1/\epsilon)}{\eta}

for
c_{1}=\max\{T_{0}+\frac{\log(\bar{\epsilon})}{c_{0}},\ \frac{1}{c_{0}}\}
.
∎

With Lemma
J.1
-
J.4
, we are able to pick some constants to facilitate the analysis below.
By decreasing
\bar{\epsilon}
and increasing
M
if needed, we can assume the existence of some
\bar{t}<\infty,\bar{\delta}<\bar{\epsilon}
such that

•

(Due to Lemma
J.1
and
J.2
)
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{j}\in\{1,2,\cdots,m\}^{k}
such that
\mathcal{J}_{\text{type}}^{\downarrow}(\bm{j})<J_{\mathcal{G}}
,
any
x\in B(\bm{0},100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon})
,
any
\bm{t}^{(k)}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{k})\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}_{+},\bm{w}^{(k)}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k})\in\mathcal{A}^{\text{type}}_{\bm{j}}
with

\displaystyle\bm{d}\big(\widetilde{\bm{x}}(\sum_{j=1}^{k}t_{j},x;\ \bm{t}^{(k)},\bm{w}^{(k)}),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)<100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon},

it holds that

\displaystyle t_{j}<\bar{t}\ \ \forall j=2,3,\cdots,k\text{ and }\left\lVert w_{j}\right\rVert

\displaystyle>\bar{\delta}\ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,k.

(J.17)

•

For any
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
with
\mathcal{J}(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})<J_{\mathcal{G}}
,

\displaystyle\bm{d}\big(\mathcal{G}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}),\mathcal{G}^{c})>100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon};

(J.18)

•

There exists some
x\in\mathcal{G}(\bm{i}^{*})
such that

\displaystyle\bm{d}(x,\mathcal{G})>100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon};

(J.19)

•

(Due to Lemma
J.3
) The constant
\bar{\epsilon}>0
is sufficiently small such that

\displaystyle\sup_{s\geq 0}\bm{d}\big(\widetilde{\bm{x}}(s,x;\ \bm{t}^{(k)},\bm{w}^{(k)}),\mathcal{G}^{c}\big)>100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon}

(J.20)

for all
x\in B(\bm{0},100l^{*}\bar{\epsilon})
, all
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
with
\mathcal{J}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})<J_{\mathcal{G}}
and
k=\sum_{j=1}^{m}i_{j}
, and all
\bm{w}^{(k)}\in\mathcal{A}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}),\ \bm{t}^{(k)}\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k-1}_{+}
;

•

(Due to Lemma
J.4
) The constant
\bar{t}<\infty
is large enough so that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\bar{\mathcal{G}}}\tau^{\eta}_{\text{ODE}}(x,\bar{\epsilon})<\bar{t}/\eta.

J.2
Bounding the distance between ODE flow and SGD iterates

Moving on, we show that, without large jumps, the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}(x)
are unlikely to show significant deviation from the deterministic gradient descent process
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(x)
defined as

\displaystyle\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=x,

(J.21)

\displaystyle\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)-\eta\nabla f\Big(\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)\Big).

(J.22)

We are ready to state the first lemma, where we bound the distance between the gradient descent iterates
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{n}(y)
and the ODE
\textbf{x}^{\eta}(t,x)
when the initial conditions
x,y
are close enough.

Lemma J.5
.

Given
t>0
and
x,y\in\mathcal{G}
such that the line segment between
\textbf{x}^{\eta}(k,x)
and
\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{k}(y)
lies in
\mathcal{G}
for any
k=1,2,\cdots,\lfloor t\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\left\lVert\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y)\right\rVert\leq(2\eta M+\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert)\exp(\eta Mt)

where
M\in(0,\infty)
is the constant in
eq.

J.8
eq.

J.9
.

Proof.

Define a continuous-time process
\textbf{y}^{\eta}(s;y)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y)
, and note that

\displaystyle\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)

\displaystyle=\textbf{x}^{\eta}(\lfloor s\rfloor,x)-\eta\int_{\lfloor s\rfloor}^{s}\nabla f(\textbf{x}^{\eta}(u,x))du

\displaystyle\textbf{x}^{\eta}(\lfloor s\rfloor,x)

\displaystyle=x-\eta\int_{0}^{\lfloor s\rfloor}\nabla f(\textbf{x}^{\eta}(u,x))du

\displaystyle\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y)=\textbf{y}^{\eta}(\lfloor s\rfloor,y)

\displaystyle=y-\eta\int_{0}^{\lfloor s\rfloor}\nabla f(\textbf{y}^{\eta}(u,y))du.

Therefore, if we define function

b(u)=\textbf{x}^{\eta}(u,x)-\textbf{y}^{\eta}(u,y),

from the fact
\left\lVert\nabla f(\cdot)\right\rVert\leq M
, one can see that
\left\lVert b(u)\right\rVert\leq\eta M+\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert
for any
u\in[0,1)
and
\left\lVert b(1)\right\rVert\leq 2\eta M+\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert
. In case that
s>1
, from the display above and the fact
\left\lVert\nabla^{2}f(\cdot)\right\rVert\leq M
, we now have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(x)-\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert b({\lfloor s\rfloor})\right\rVert+\eta M;

\displaystyle\left\lVert b({\lfloor s\rfloor})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\eta M\int_{1}^{\lfloor s\rfloor}\left\lVert b(u)\right\rVert du.

From Gronwall’s inequality (see Theorem 68, Chapter V of
Protter 2005
, where we let function
\alpha(u)
be
\alpha(u)=\left\lVert b(u+1)\right\rVert
), we have

\left\lVert\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(x)-\textbf{x}^{\eta}(s,x)\right\rVert\leq(2\eta M+\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert)\exp(\eta Mt).

This concludes the proof.
∎

Now we consider an extension of the previous Lemma in the following sense:
when both perturbed by at most
l^{*}
similar perturbations, the ODE flow and gradient descent process should still stay close enough.
Analogous to the definition of the perturbed ODE
\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}
, we can construct a process
\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}
as a perturbed gradient descent process as follows.
For some integer
1\leq l\leq l^{*}
,
a sequence of strictly positive integers
(t_{2},\cdots,t_{l})
(with convention
t_{1}=0
and let
\bm{t}^{(l)}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l})
),
a sequence of non-zero vectors
\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)}=(\widetilde{w}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{w}_{l})

and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the perturbed gradient descent iterates with gradient clipping at
b
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{n}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{n-1}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})+\varphi_{b}\big(-\eta\nabla f(\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{n-1}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)}))+\sum_{j=2}^{l}\mathbbm{1}\{n=t_{1}+\cdots+t_{j}\}\widetilde{w}_{j}\big)

(J.23)

with initial condition
\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{0}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})=y+\varphi_{b}(\widetilde{w}_{1})
.

Corollary J.6
.

For any fixed
\epsilon>0,t>0
, the following claim holds for all
\eta>0
sufficiently small: given any
x,y\in\mathcal{G}
,
some integer
1\leq l\leq l^{*}
,
a sequence of strictly positive integers
\bm{t}^{(l)}=(t_{j})_{j=2}^{l}
(with convention
t_{1}=0
),
and any two sequences of vectors
\bm{w}^{(l)}=(w_{j})_{j=1}^{l},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)}=(\widetilde{w}_{j})_{j\geq 1}^{l}
such that (let
t_{\text{end}}=\sum_{j=1}^{l}t_{j}
)

•

|x-y|<\epsilon
;

•

t_{j}\leq 2t/\eta
for all
j\in[l]
;

•

|w_{j}-\widetilde{w}_{j}|<\epsilon
for all
j\in[l]
;

•

The line segment between
\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(k,x;\bm{t}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})
and
\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{k}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})
lies in
\mathcal{G}
for any
k\leq t_{\text{end}}-1
,

then we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t_{\text{end}}]}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,x;\bm{t}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})-\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})\right\rVert\leq\bar{\rho}(t)\epsilon

where the constant
\bar{\rho}=(2\exp(2Mt)+2)^{l^{*}}
. In particular,
\bar{\rho}=\bar{\rho}(\bar{t})
is a constant where
\bar{t}
is the constant in
eq.

J.11
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, fix some
\eta\in(0,\epsilon/2M)
. We will show that for any
\eta
in the range the claim would hold.

First, on interval
[0,t_{2})
, from Lemma
J.5
, one can see that (since
2M\eta<\epsilon
)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t_{2})}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,x;\bm{s}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})-\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})\right\rVert\leq 2\exp(2Mt)\cdot\epsilon.

The at
t=t_{2}
, by considering the difference between
w_{2}
and
\widetilde{w}_{2}
, and the possible change due to one more gradient descent step (which is bounded by
\eta M<\epsilon
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t_{2}]}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,x;\bm{s}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})-\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})\right\rVert\leq(2\exp(2Mt)+2)\cdot\epsilon.

Now we proceed inductively. For any
j=2,3,\cdots,l-1
, assume that

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t_{j}]}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,x;\bm{s}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})-\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})\right\rVert\leq(2\exp(2Mt)+2)^{j-1}\cdot\epsilon.

Then by focusing on interval
[t_{j},t_{j+1}]
and using Lemma
J.5
again, one can show that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[t_{j},t_{j+1}]}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,x;\bm{s}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})-\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon+\big((2\exp(2Mt)+2)^{j-1}+1\big)\exp(2Mt)\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq(2\exp(2Mt)+2)^{j}\cdot\epsilon.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Using an almost identical approach, one can show the following results that bounds the distance between two ODE flows with similar initial values and jumps.

Corollary J.7
.

There exists some constant
\rho^{*}\in(0,\infty)

such that the following claim holds:
For any fixed
\epsilon>0
,
any
x,y\in\mathcal{G}
,
any integer
1\leq l\leq l^{*}
,
a sequence of strictly positive integers
\bm{t}^{(l)}=(t_{j})_{j=2}^{l}
(with convention
t_{1}=0
),
and any two sequences of vectors
\bm{w}^{(l)}=(w_{j})_{j=1}^{l},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)}=(\widetilde{w}_{j})_{j\geq 1}^{l}
such that (let
t_{\text{end}}=\sum_{j=1}^{l}t_{j}
)

•

|x-y|<\epsilon
;

•

t_{j}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta
for all
j\in[l]
;

•

|w_{j}-\widetilde{w}_{j}|<\epsilon
for all
j\in[l]
;

•

The line segment between
\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,x;\bm{t}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})
and
\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})
lies in
\mathcal{G}
for any
s<t_{\text{end}}
,

then we have

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[0,t_{\text{end}}]}\left\lVert\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,x;\bm{t}^{(l)},\bm{w}^{(l)})-\widetilde{\bm{x}}^{\eta}(s,y;\bm{t}^{(l)},\widetilde{\bm{w}}^{(l)})\right\rVert\leq\rho^{*}\epsilon.

In the next few results, we show that a similar type of control can be obtained on the distance between gradient descent iterates
\widetilde{\bm{y}}^{\eta}_{n}
and the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}
.
Specifically, our first goal is to show that before any
large
jump, it is unlikely that the (deterministic) gradient descent process
\bm{y}^{\eta}_{n}
would deviate too far from
X^{\eta}_{n}
.
To facilitate the analysis below, we introduce some additional notations.
First, we will group the noises
Z_{n}
based on a threshold level
\delta>0
: let us define

\displaystyle Z_{n}^{\leq\delta,\eta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\eta\left\lVert Z_{n}\right\rVert\leq\delta\},

(J.24)

\displaystyle Z_{n}^{>\delta,\eta}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\eta\left\lVert Z_{n}\right\rVert>\delta\}.

(J.25)

The former are viewed as
small noises
while the latter will be referred to as
large noises
or
large jumps
.
Furthermore, for any
j\geq 1
, define the
j
th
arrival time, size, and direction of large jumps as

\displaystyle T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n>T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta):\ \eta\left\lVert Z_{n}\right\rVert>\delta\},\quad T_{0}^{\eta}(\delta)=0,

(J.26)

\displaystyle W^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)},

(J.27)

\displaystyle\Theta^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}W^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)/\left\lVert W^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)\right\rVert.

(J.28)

The following event

\displaystyle A(n,\eta,\epsilon,\delta)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\max_{k=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)}\eta\left\lVert Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{k}\right\rVert\leq\epsilon\Big\}

(J.29)

describes a scenario where, before the first large jump, not much perturbation has been caused by noises.

Lemma J.8
.

For any
\epsilon>0,\eta>0
,
any
x,y\in\mathcal{G}
and positive integer
n
such that
\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert<\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta Mn)}
, on event

\displaystyle A(n,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta Mn)},\delta)\bigcap

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Big\{\text{the line segment between }\textbf{y}^{\eta}_{j}(y)\text{ and }X^{\eta}_{j}(x)\text{ lies in }\mathcal{G}\ \ \forall j=0,1,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)\Big\},

we have

\left\lVert\bm{y}^{\eta}_{m}(y)-X^{\eta}_{m}(x)\right\rVert<\epsilon\ \ \forall m=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1).

Proof.

On the said event, we are able to apply Lemma
G.5
and obtain that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{y}^{\eta}_{j}(y)-X^{\eta}_{j}(x)\right\rVert\leq(\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert+\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta Mn)})\exp(\eta Mj)<\epsilon\ \ \ \forall j=1,2,\cdots,n\wedge(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)-1)

and conclude the proof.
∎

Similar to the extension from Lemma
J.5
to Corollary
J.6
, we can extend Lemma
J.8
to show that, if we consider the a gradient descent process that is only perturbed by large noises, then it should stay pretty close to the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}
. To be specific, let

\displaystyle Y^{\eta}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=x

\displaystyle Y^{\eta}_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=Y^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)-\varphi_{b}\big(-\eta\nabla f\big(Y^{\eta}_{n-1}(x)\big)+\sum_{j\geq 1}\mathbbm{1}\{n=T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)\}\eta Z_{n}\big).

be a gradient descent process (with gradient clipping at threshold
b
) that is only perturbed by large noises. The next corollary can be shown by an approach identical to the one for Corollary
J.6
(namely, inductively repeating Lemma
J.8
at arrival time of each jump) so we omit the details here.

Corollary J.9
.

There exists some function
\widetilde{\rho}:(0,\infty)\mapsto(0,\infty)
such that the following claim hold for all
\epsilon>0,t>0
and all sufficiently small
\eta>0
: For any
x,y\in\mathcal{G})
with
\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert<\epsilon
and any
l\in[l^{*}]
, on event
A_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta,l,t)\cap B_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta,l,t)\cap C_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta,l,t)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert Y^{\eta}_{n}(y)-X^{\eta}_{n}(x)\right\rVert<\widetilde{\rho}(t)\epsilon\ \ \forall n=1,2,\cdots,T^{\eta}_{l}(\delta)

where

\displaystyle A_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta,l,t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\Big\{\forall i=1,\cdots,l,\ \max_{j=T^{\eta}_{i-1}(\delta)+1,\cdots,T^{\eta}_{i}(\delta)-1}\eta\left\lVert Z_{T^{\eta}_{i-1}(\delta)+1}+\cdots+Z_{j}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(2tM)}\Big\};

\displaystyle B_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta,l,t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\Big\{\forall j=2,\cdots,l,T^{\eta}_{j}(\delta)-T^{\eta}_{j-1}(\delta)\leq 2t/\eta\Big\},

\displaystyle C_{0}(\epsilon,\eta,\delta,l,t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\{\text{the line segment between }Y^{\eta}_{j}(y)\text{ and }X^{\eta}_{j}(x)\text{ lies in }\mathcal{G}\ \ \forall j=0,1,\cdots,T^{\eta}_{l}(\delta)-1\}.

In particular,
\widetilde{\rho}=\widetilde{\rho}(\bar{t})
is a constant

The next result shows that the type of events
A(n,\eta,\epsilon,\delta)
defined in
eq.

J.29
is indeed very likely to occur, especially for small
\epsilon
. For clarity of the presentation, we introduce the following definitions that are slightly more general than the
small
and
large
jumps defined in
eq.

J.24
eq.

J.25
(for any
c>0
)

\displaystyle Z^{\leq c}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\left\lVert Z_{n}\right\rVert\leq c\},

\displaystyle Z^{>c}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\left\lVert Z_{n}\right\rVert>c\}.

Lemma J.10
.

Given any
t>0
,
\epsilon>0
, and
N>0
, the following holds for any sufficiently small
\delta>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{n}\right\rVert>\epsilon\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

as
\eta\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

For any
C\in(0,\infty),\delta>0,\beta\in(0,1)
, consider the following decomposition of the small noise
Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{n}
. Here we adopt the convention that
E_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\char 92\relax(\cup_{j=1}^{m}E_{j})
. (For definitions and properties of the closed cones
E_{1},\cdots,E_{m}
, see Assumption
I.6
and the remark underneath.)

\displaystyle Z^{(j)}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{Z_{n}\in E_{j}\},

\displaystyle Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z^{(j)}_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\left\lVert Z^{(j)}_{n}\right\rVert\leq C\},

\displaystyle Z^{\downarrow,(j)}_{C,\delta,\eta}(n)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z^{(j)}_{n}\mathbbm{1}\{\left\lVert Z^{(j)}_{n}\right\rVert\in(C,\delta/\eta]\}

Based on this decomposition, we define the following iid random variable sequences

\displaystyle\widetilde{z}^{(j)}(n)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{n}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{n},

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\left\lVert Z^{\downarrow,(j)}_{C,\delta,\eta}(n)\right\rVert,

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta}(n)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\left\lVert Z^{\leq 1/\eta^{\beta},(j)}_{n}\right\rVert.

Meanwhile, define the projection operators
\textbf{P}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
with
\textbf{P}_{i}(v_{1},\cdots,v_{d})=v_{i}
. First, recall that
\mathbb{E}Z_{1}=\bm{0}
. So for all
C
sufficiently large, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{y\geq C}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq y}_{1}\right\rVert=\sup_{y\geq C}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}Z^{>y}_{1}\right\rVert<\frac{\epsilon}{2t}.

Moreover, due to
H_{j}\in RV_{-(\alpha_{j}+1)}
for all
j=1,2,\cdots,m
and
H_{0}(x)=o(1/x^{\alpha})
for any
\alpha>0
(for definition of functions
H_{j}
, see
eq.

I.5
), we know that
\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Z^{(j)}_{n}\right\rVert<\infty
for any
j=0,1,\cdots,m
, implying that for all
C
large enough, we have (for any
j=0,1,\cdots,m
)

\displaystyle\sup_{y,z\geq C}\left\lVert\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq y,(j)}_{1}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq z,(j)}_{1}\right\rVert<\frac{1}{2t}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{24\sqrt{m+1}}.

(J.30)

Fix such
C
. Besides, recall that in Assumption
I.6
we have
1<\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\cdot<\alpha_{m}
. Therefore, we are able fix some
\beta\in(0,1)
and
\delta>0
so that

\displaystyle\beta\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle>1,

(J.31)

\displaystyle(N+1)\delta

\displaystyle<\frac{\epsilon}{12\sqrt{m+1}},

(J.32)

\displaystyle\big(\frac{N}{\beta\alpha_{1}-1}+1\big)\delta

\displaystyle<\frac{\epsilon}{12\sqrt{m+1}}.

(J.33)

With the fixed
C
and
\delta
, note that eventually
\delta/\eta>C
as
\eta\downarrow 0
. Therefore, for all
\eta
sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{n}\right\rVert>\epsilon\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{k}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{k}\right\rVert>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{k}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{k}+Z^{\downarrow,(j)}_{C,\delta,\eta}(k)+\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{k}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq\delta/\eta,(j)}_{k}\right\rVert>\frac{\epsilon}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{k}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{k}+Z^{\downarrow,(j)}_{C,\delta,\eta}(k)+\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq C,(j)}_{k}-\mathbb{E}Z^{\leq\delta/\eta,(j)}_{k}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ >\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{m+1}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert\sum_{k=1}^{n}\widetilde{z}^{(j)}(k)\right\rVert>\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{m+1}}\Big)

(J.34)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{m}\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(k)>\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{m+1}}\Big).

(J.35)

We bound the two terms
eq.

J.34
and
eq.

J.35
respectively. First, observe that

eq.

J.34

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\big|\sum_{k=1}^{n}\textbf{P}_{i}\big(\widetilde{z}^{(j)}(k)\big)\big|>\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{(m+1)d}}\Big).

Let
\widetilde{z}^{(j)}_{i}(k)=\textbf{P}_{i}\big(\widetilde{z}^{(j)}(k)\big)
and let
\widetilde{z}^{(j)}_{i}
be an iid copy. By definition,
|\widetilde{z}^{(j)}_{i}|\leq 2C
. Then from Hoeffding’s inequality,

eq.

J.34

\displaystyle\leq 2(m+1)d\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\big(\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{d(m+1)}}\big)^{2}\cdot\frac{1/\eta^{2}}{2C\lceil t/\eta\rceil\ }\Big)=o(\eta^{N}).

As for term
eq.

J.35
, w.l.o.g. let us fix some
j=0,1,\cdots,m
and bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)>\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{m+1}}\Big)

(J.36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)>\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{m+1}},\ I=i\Big)}_{\triangleq\text{(I)}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)>\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{m+1}},\ I\geq k\Big)}_{\triangleq\text{(II)}}

where
I\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\#\{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil:\ \widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)>1/\eta^{\beta}\}

and
k
is some positive integer such that

k\delta<\frac{\epsilon}{12\sqrt{m+1}},\ \ k(\beta\alpha_{1}-1)>N.

Note that we can find such
k
due to our choice of constants in
eq.

J.31
-
eq.

J.33
.

For (I) and any
i=0,1,\cdots,k-1
, observe that (the non-negative RVs
\widetilde{Z}_{\beta}(n)
and
\widetilde{Z}(n)
are defined at the beginning of the proof)

(I)

\displaystyle\leq{\lceil t/\eta\rceil\choose i}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil-i}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)>\frac{\epsilon}{6\sqrt{m+1}}-i\eta\cdot\frac{\delta}{\eta},\ \widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)\leq 1/\eta^{\beta}\ \forall n\in[\lceil t/\eta\rceil-i]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil^{i}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil-i}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)>\frac{\epsilon}{12\sqrt{m+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\eta},\ \widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)\leq 1/\eta^{\beta}\ \forall n\in[\lceil t/\eta\rceil-i]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil^{i}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta}(n)>\frac{\epsilon}{12\sqrt{m+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}\Big).

Let
\widetilde{W}^{(j)}(n)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta}(n)-\mathbb{E}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta}(n)
and let
\widetilde{W}^{(j)}
be an iid copy.
Due to
eq.

J.30
, for all
\eta
sufficiently small we will have that
\lceil t/\eta\rceil|\mathbb{E}\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta}(1)|<\frac{\epsilon}{24\sqrt{m+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}
. Therefore, for all
\eta
sufficiently small,

(I)

\displaystyle\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil^{i}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(|\sum_{n=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\widetilde{W}^{(j)}(n)|>\frac{\epsilon}{24\sqrt{m+1}}\cdot\frac{1}{\eta}\Big).

(J.37)

Observe that
|\widetilde{W}^{(j)}|\leq 2/\eta^{\beta}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{W}^{(j)})^{2}=var{\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta}}\leq\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta})^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}2y\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta}>y)dy=\int_{0}^{1/\eta^{\beta}}2yH_{j}(y)dy.

Note that there are only two possibilities:

•

If
j\neq 0
and
\alpha_{j}\leq 2
, then from Karamata’s theorem, we have
\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta})^{2}\in RV_{-\beta(2-\alpha_{j})}(\eta)
and note that
\beta(2-\alpha_{j})<1
;

•

Otherwise, one can find some
\Delta>0
such that
H_{j}(x)=o(1/x^{2+\Delta})
, implying that
\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}_{\beta})^{2}\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}2yH_{j}(y)dy<\infty
regardless of the actual value of
\eta>0
.

In any case, using Bernstein’s inequality, we can conclude that

eq.

J.37

\displaystyle\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil^{i}\cdot 2\exp\big(-\frac{\frac{1}{2}\big(\frac{\epsilon}{12\sqrt{m+1}}\big)^{2}/\eta^{2}}{\lceil t/\eta\rceil\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{W}^{(j)})^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\big(\frac{\epsilon}{12\sqrt{m+1}}\big)/\eta^{1-\beta}}\big)=o(\eta^{N}).

On the other hand,

(II)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(I\geq k)\leq{\lceil t/\eta\rceil\choose k}\cdot\mathbb{P}\Big(\widetilde{Z}^{(j)}(n)>1/\eta^{\beta}\ \forall n=1,\ldots,k\Big)\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil^{k}\cdot\Big(H_{j}(1/\eta^{\beta})\Big)^{k},

which is upper bounded by a function regularly varying w.r.t.
\eta
with index
k\big(\beta\alpha_{1}-1\big)>N
.
Therefore,
\text{(II)}=o(\eta^{N})
and we conclude the proof.
∎

J.3
Analyzing different scenarios before the first exit or return

Using results and arguments above, we are able to illustrate the typical behavior of the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}
in the following two scenarios. First, we show that, when starting from anywhere in
\mathcal{G}
, the SGD iterates
X^{\eta}_{n}

will most likely return to the neighborhood of the
\bm{0}
within a short period of time without exiting
\Omega
.

Lemma J.11
.

For any
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon})
, the following claim holds:

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\inf_{x:\bm{d}(x,\mathcal{G}^{c})>\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{n}\in\mathcal{G}\ \forall n\leq T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\text{and }T_{\mathrm{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\leq\rho(\epsilon)/\eta\Big)=1

where the stopping time involved is defined as

\displaystyle T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}\min\{n\geq 0:\left\lVert X^{\eta}_{n}\right\rVert<2\epsilon\}

and the function
\rho(\epsilon)=c_{1}+c_{1}\log(2/\epsilon)
with
c_{1}\in(0,\infty)
being the constant specified in Lemma
J.4
.

Proof.

Let
t=\rho(\epsilon)=c_{1}+c_{1}\log(2/\epsilon)
and
n=\lceil t/\eta\rceil

and arbitrarily choose some
x
with
\bm{d}(x,\mathcal{G}^{c})>\epsilon.

Also, fix some
N>0
.
Due to Lemma
J.10
, we are able to pick some
\delta>0
such that (note that
\eta n\leq 2t
eventually as
\eta\downarrow 0
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{n}\right\rVert>\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(2\eta nM)}\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

First, from Lemma
J.5
we know that for any
\eta
small enough such that

\displaystyle 2\eta M\exp(2tM)<\epsilon/2,

we must have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{x}^{\eta}(s,x)-\bm{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(x)\right\rVert\leq\epsilon/2\ \ \ \forall s\in[0,n].

Next, using Lemma
J.8
, we see that on event

\displaystyle A(n,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta nM)},\delta)\bigcap\{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>\lceil t/\eta\rceil\},

we must have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{y}^{\eta}_{k}(x)-X^{\eta}_{k}(x)\right\rVert<\epsilon\ \ \ \forall k=0,1,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil.

Combining these facts with the Lemma
J.4
, we have shown that, for all
\eta\in(0,\frac{\epsilon}{4M\exp(2tM)})
, on event
A(n,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta nM)},\delta)\bigcap\{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>\lceil t/\eta\rceil\}
we must have
X^{\eta}_{n}\in\mathcal{G}\ \forall n\leq T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\text{ and }T_{\mathrm{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\leq\rho(\epsilon)/\eta
.
In other words,

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\sup_{x:\bm{d}(x,\mathcal{G}^{c})>\epsilon}\mathbb{P}_{x}\Big(X^{\eta}_{n}\notin\mathcal{G}\ \text{for some }n\leq T_{\text{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)\text{ OR }T_{\mathrm{return}}(\eta,\epsilon)>\rho(\epsilon)/\eta\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}\big(A(n,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta nM)},\delta)^{c}\big)+\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil).

However, our choice of
\delta
at the beginning of the proof implies that
\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}\big(A(n,\eta,\frac{\epsilon}{2\exp(\eta nM)},\delta)^{c}\big)=0
.
Meanwhile, from Assumption
I.6
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil)=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\big(1-\sum_{j=0}^{m}H_{j}(\delta/\eta)\big)^{k-1}\cdot(\sum_{j=0}^{m}H_{j}(\delta/\eta))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(m+1)H_{1}(\delta/\eta)\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\big(1-H_{1}(\delta/\eta)\big)^{k-1}

for all
\eta
sufficiently small (due to regularly varying nature of
H_{j}
and
1<\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{m}
). In particular, since
H_{1}\in RV_{-\alpha_{1}}
, for any fixed
\Delta\in(0,\alpha_{1}-1)
, we have (for all
\eta
sufficiently small),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil)\leq

\displaystyle(m+1)\eta^{\alpha_{1}-\Delta}\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}(1-\eta^{\alpha_{1}+\Delta})^{k-1}=\frac{m+1}{\eta^{2\Delta}}\mathbb{P}(U(\eta)\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil)

where
U(\eta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.08144pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}}\hskip-2.08144pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Geom(\eta^{\alpha_{1}+\Delta})
.
Lemma
G.4
then tells us that, for all
\eta
sufficiently small,
\mathbb{P}(U(\eta)\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil)\leq\frac{2}{t}\eta^{\alpha_{1}-1+\Delta}
, thus implying

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil)\leq\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{2(m+1)}{t}\eta^{\alpha_{1}-1-\Delta}=0

and concluding the proof.
∎

In the next result, we show that, once entering a
\epsilon-
small neighborhood of the local minimum, the SGD iterates will most likely stay there until the next large jump.

Lemma J.12
.

Given
N_{0}>0
, the following claim holds for all
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\epsilon}/3)
and all
\delta>0
that is sufficiently small:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in{B(\bm{0},2\epsilon)}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists n<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\ s.t.\ \left\lVert X^{\eta}_{n}(x)\right\rVert>3\epsilon\Big)=o(\eta^{N_{0}})

as
\eta\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

Let
t=c_{1}+c_{1}\log(1/\epsilon)
where
c_{1}\in(0,\infty)
is the constant specified in Lemma
J.4
, and
n=\lceil t/\eta\rceil
.
Fix some
\beta>1+\alpha_{1}
and
N>\beta-1+N_{0}
.
Due to Lemma
J.10
, the following claim holds for all
\delta
that is sufficiently small:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{k=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{k}\right\rVert>\frac{\epsilon/2}{2\exp(2\eta nM)}\Big)=o(\eta^{N})

We fix one of such
\delta
.
Therefore, for event

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}

\displaystyle\Big\{\exists j\leq\lceil\frac{1/\eta^{\beta}}{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\rceil\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \max_{k=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{1+(j-1)\lceil t/\eta\rceil}+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{2+(j-1)\lceil t/\eta\rceil}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{k}\right\rVert>\frac{\epsilon/2}{2\exp(2\eta nM)}\Big\},

the iid nature of noises
(Z_{n})_{n\geq 1}
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{A})\leq

\displaystyle(1+\frac{1}{t\eta^{\beta-1}})\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{k=1,2,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\eta\left\lVert Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{1}+\cdots+Z^{\leq\delta/\eta}_{k}\right\rVert>\frac{\epsilon/2}{2\exp(2\eta nM)}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle o(\eta^{N-\beta+1})\ \ \text{as }\eta\downarrow 0.

Meanwhile,
Note that
T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq T^{\eta,(1)}_{1}(\delta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\min\{n\geq 0:\ \eta|Z_{n}|>\delta,\ Z_{n}\in E_{1}\}
.
Therefore,

\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>1/\eta^{\beta})\leq\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta,(1)}_{1}(\delta)>1/\eta^{\beta})
.
Specifically, note that
T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.08144pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle d$}}\hskip-2.08144pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}{}Geom(H_{1}(\delta/\eta))
and
H_{1}\in RV_{-\alpha_{1}}
.
Lemma
G.3
then tells us the existence of some
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>1/\eta^{\beta})=o\big(\exp(-c/\eta)\big)

as
\eta\downarrow 0
. In summary, we have established that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{A}\cup\{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>1/\eta^{\beta}\})=o(\eta^{N_{0}}).

Now let us focus on the complementary event
(\widetilde{A})^{c}\cap\{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq 1/\eta^{\beta}\}
. On this event, there must be some
j\leq\lceil\frac{1/\eta^{\beta}}{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}\rceil
such that
1+(j-1)\lceil t/\eta\rceil\leq T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq j\lceil t/\eta\rceil
.

Let us arbitrarily choose some
x\in B(\bm{0},2\epsilon)
. If
j=1
, then due to
eq.

J.3
, we know that
\bm{x}^{\eta}(s,x)\in B(\bm{0},2\epsilon)
for all
s\geq 0
. Also, from Lemma
J.5
we know that for any
\eta
small enough such that
2\eta M\exp(2tM)<\epsilon/2,

we must have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{x}^{\eta}(s,x)-\bm{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(x)\right\rVert\leq\epsilon/2\ \ \ \forall s\in[0,\lceil t/\eta\rceil].

Besides, on event
(\widetilde{A})^{c}\cup\{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\leq 1/\eta^{\beta}\}\cap\{j=1\}
, due to Lemma
J.8
,
we must have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{y}^{\eta}_{k}(x)-X^{\eta}_{k}(x)\right\rVert<\epsilon/2\ \ \ \forall k=0,1,\cdots,T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)

with
T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)<\lceil t/\eta\rceil.
In conclusion, in the case that
j=1
, we must have
\left\lVert X^{\eta}_{n}(x)\right\rVert<3\epsilon
for all
n<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)
.

Now consider the case with
j\geq 2
. Similarly, due to Lemma
J.5
and
J.8
, we now have that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{x}^{\eta}(s,x)-\bm{y}^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor}(x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon/2\ \ \ \forall s\in[0,\lceil t/\eta\rceil],

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{y}^{\eta}_{k}(x)-X^{\eta}_{k}(x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle<\epsilon/2\ \ \ \forall k=0,1,\cdots,\lceil t/\eta\rceil,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{x}^{\eta}(s,x)\right\rVert

\displaystyle<2\epsilon\ \ \forall s\in[0,\lceil t/\eta\rceil].

In particular, due to our choice of
t
at the beginning of the proof and Lemma
J.4
, one can see that
\left\lVert\bm{x}^{\eta}(\lceil t/\eta\rceil,x)\right\rVert<\epsilon.
Collecting all the results, we now know that

\displaystyle\left\lVert X^{\eta}_{k}(x)\right\rVert<3\epsilon\ \forall k\leq\lceil t/\eta\rceil,\ \ \left\lVert X^{\eta}_{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}(x)\right\rVert<2\epsilon.

Now it suffices to apply the repeated apply the same arguments above. For instance, if
j=2
, then by letting
x_{1}=X^{\eta}_{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}(x)
and bounding the gap between trajectory of
X^{\eta}_{\lfloor s\rfloor+\lceil t/\eta\rceil}(x)
and
\bm{x}^{\eta}(s,x_{1})
using Lemma
J.5
and
J.8
, one can show that
\left\lVert X^{\eta}_{n}(x)\right\rVert<3\epsilon
for all
n<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)
(since in the case of
j=2
, we have
T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)<2\lceil t/\eta\rceil
). Otherwise, we have
j\geq 3
and the same arguments above can be applied to show that

\displaystyle\left\lVert X^{\eta}_{k}(x)\right\rVert<3\epsilon\ \forall k\leq 2\lceil t/\eta\rceil,\ \ \left\lVert X^{\eta}_{\lceil t/\eta\rceil}(x)\right\rVert<2\epsilon,,\ \ \left\lVert X^{\eta}_{2\lceil t/\eta\rceil}(x)\right\rVert<2\epsilon.

In summary, by proceeding inductively, we establish that for all
\eta\in(0,\frac{\epsilon}{4M\exp(2tM)})
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B(\bm{0},2\epsilon)}\mathbb{P}\Big(\exists n<T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)\ s.t.\ \left\lVert X^{\eta}_{n}(x)\right\rVert>3\epsilon\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{A}\cup\{T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta)>1/\eta^{\beta}\})

and this concludes the proof.
∎

We introduce a few concepts that will be crucial to the analysis below. Recall the definition of ODE with jumps
\widetilde{\bm{x}}

(note that we will drop the notational dependency on learning rate
\eta
when we choose
\eta=1
).
Recall the definitions of
\bm{i}^{*},k^{*}
(see Assumption
I.7
and
I.8
and the remarks underneath). We define the following mapping
h
from
\bm{r}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{k^{*}}),\bm{\theta}=(\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{k^{*}})\in(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{k^{*}},\bm{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{k^{*}})\in\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}_{+}
such that

\displaystyle h(\bm{r},\bm{\theta},\bm{t})=\widetilde{\bm{x}}(\sum_{i=1}^{k^{*}}t_{i},\bm{0};\ \bm{t},\bm{w})

(J.38)

where
\bm{w}=(w_{i})_{i=1}^{k^{*}}
with
w_{i}=r_{i}\theta_{i}
.
From the continuity of the ODE flow, we see that
h
is a continuous mapping.
Also, we introduce the concept of
type
for configuration
\bm{i}^{*}
. Specifically, define

\displaystyle\bm{j}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{m})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\big\{(j_{1},\cdots,j_{k^{*}})\in\{0,1,2,\cdots,m\}^{k^{*}}:\ \#\{n:\ j_{n}=k\}=i_{k}\ \forall k\in[m]\big\}

(J.39)

and for any
\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})
, we say that
\bm{w}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{k^{*}})\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{i}^{*})
has type
\bm{j}
if

\displaystyle w_{i}\in E_{j_{i}}\ \forall i\in[k^{*}].

In other words, based on the direction of each jump in
\bm{w}\in\mathcal{A}(\bm{i}^{*})
we group them into different types. Note that
|\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})|<\infty
.
Now for any type
\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})
, define a (Borel) measure on
\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}_{+}\times(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{k^{*}}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}-1}_{+}
as

\displaystyle\mu_{\bm{j}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\nu_{\alpha_{\bm{j}_{i}}})\times(\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}S_{\bm{j}_{i}})\times\textbf{Leb}^{k^{*}-1}

(J.40)

where, for any
\alpha>0
, the measure
\nu_{\alpha}
is the Borel measure on
(0,\infty)
with
\nu_{\alpha}(x,\infty)=1/x^{\alpha}
.
We use the concepts above to characterize behavior of large noises in
(Z_{n})_{n\geq 1}.

Definition J.1
.

For any
\eta,\delta>0,\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})
and any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we say noise
Z_{n}
is of
type-
(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})
iff

•

\eta\left\lVert Z_{n}\right\rVert>\delta
;

•

For
\widetilde{t}_{1}=n
and
\widetilde{t}_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\min\{k>\widetilde{t}_{i-1}:\ \eta\left\lVert Z_{k}\right\rVert>\delta\}
for all
i=2,3,\cdots,k^{*}
, it holds that
\widetilde{t}_{i}-\widetilde{t}_{i-1}<2\bar{t}/\eta
for all
i=2,3,\cdots,k^{*}
;

•

For
t_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\widetilde{t}_{i}-\widetilde{t}_{i-1}
(with
t_{1}=0
),
w_{i}=\eta Z_{\widetilde{t}_{i}}
, and

\displaystyle\bm{r}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(\left\lVert w_{1}\right\rVert,\left\lVert w_{2}\right\rVert,\cdots,\left\lVert w_{k^{*}}\right\rVert),

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}

\displaystyle=(w_{1}/\left\lVert w_{1}\right\rVert,\cdots,w_{k^{*}}/\left\lVert w_{k^{*}}\right\rVert),

\displaystyle\bm{t}

\displaystyle=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{k^{*}}),

it holds that
h(\bm{r},\bm{\theta},\bm{t})\in A
.

More generally, we say noise
Z_{n}
is of
type-
(A,\delta,\eta)
iff there exists
\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})
such that
Z_{n}
is of
type-
(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})
.

Due to the iid nature of
(Z_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, let us consider an iid sequence
(V_{j})_{j\geq 0}
where the sequence has the same law of
Z_{1}
.
Note that for any fixed
n\geq 1
, the probability that
Z_{n}
is of type-
(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})
is equal to the probability that
V_{0}
is of type-
(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})
.
More specifically,
let
H(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}H_{j}(x)=\mathbb{P}(\left\lVert Z_{1}\right\rVert>x)
.
Due to Assumption
I.6
,
H\in RV_{-\alpha_{1}}
.
Besides,
\mathbb{P}(\eta\left\lVert V_{0}\right\rVert>\delta)=H(\delta/\eta)
.
Now we focus on conditional probability admitting the following form:

\displaystyle p(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})=\mathbb{P}\Big(V_{0}\ \text{is of type-$(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})$}\ \Big|\ \eta\left\lVert V_{0}\right\rVert>\delta\Big).

Lemma J.13
.

For any
\delta>0
, any
\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})
, and any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

\displaystyle h(r_{1},\cdots,r_{k^{*}},\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{k^{*}},t_{2},\cdots,t_{k^{*}})\in A\ \Longrightarrow\ t_{i}<2\bar{t}\ \forall i=2,\cdots,k^{*}\text{ and }r_{i}>\delta\ \forall i\in[k^{*}],

(J.41)

it holds that

\displaystyle\mu_{\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A^{\circ})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\eta}\frac{p(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})}{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{m}\big(H_{k}(1/\eta)\big)^{i^{*}_{k}}}{H(1/\eta)\eta^{k^{*}-1}}}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\eta}\frac{p(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})}{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{m}\big(H_{k}(1/\eta)\big)^{i^{*}_{k}}}{H(1/\eta)\eta^{k^{*}-1}}}\leq\mu_{\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(\bar{A})\big).

Proof.

Let us start by fixing some notations. Let
T_{1}=0
, and define stopping times
T_{j}=\min\{n>T_{j-1}:\eta\left\lVert V_{n}\right\rVert>\delta\}
and inter-arrival times
T^{\prime}_{j}=T_{j}-T_{j-1}
for any
j\geq 2
(with
T^{\prime}_{1}=0
),
and large jump
W_{j}=V_{T_{j}}
for any
j\geq 0
.
Note that: first, the pair
(T^{\prime}_{i},W_{i})
is independent of
(T^{\prime}_{j},W_{j})
for any
i\neq j
; besides,
W_{j}
and
T^{\prime}_{j}
are independent for all
j\geq 1
.

Define the following sequence (of random elements)
\bm{w}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{l^{*}})
and
\bm{t}=(t_{1},\cdots,t_{l^{*}})
by

\displaystyle w_{i}=\eta W_{i},\ \ t_{i}=\eta T^{\prime}_{i}.

and
\bm{r}=(\textbf{T}_{r}(w_{i}))_{i=1}^{k^{*}},\bm{\theta}=(\textbf{T}_{\theta}(w_{i}))_{i=1}^{k^{*}}
where operators
\textbf{T}_{r}(x)=\left\lVert x\right\rVert,\textbf{T}_{\theta}(x)=x/\left\lVert x\right\rVert
constitute the polar coordinate transform.
By definition of type-
(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})
, we know that

•

T^{\prime}_{i}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta
for any
i=2,\cdots,k^{*}
;

•

\eta\left\lVert W_{i}\right\rVert>{\delta}
for any
i=1,2,\cdots,k^{*}
;

•

w_{i}\in E_{\bm{j}_{i}}
(i.e.
\theta_{i}\in F_{i}
) for all
i\in[k^{*}]
;

•

h(\bm{r},\bm{\theta},\bm{t})\in A
.

Therefore, (let
R_{i}=\textbf{T}_{r}(W_{i}),\Theta_{i}=\textbf{T}_{\theta}(W_{i})
)

\displaystyle p(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{2}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)\Big)^{k^{*}-1}\cdot\int\mathbbm{1}\Big\{(\bm{r},\bm{\theta},\bm{t})\in h^{-1}(A)\Big\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cdot\big(\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\mathbb{P}(W_{i}\in E_{i})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\eta R_{i}=dr_{i},\ \Theta_{i}=d\theta_{i})\big)\cdot\big(\prod_{i=2}^{k^{*}}\mathbb{P}(\eta T^{\prime}_{i}=dt_{i}\ |\ \eta T^{\prime}_{i}\leq 2\bar{t})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{2}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)\Big)^{k^{*}-1}\cdot\big[\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\mathbb{P}(W_{i}\in E_{i})\big]\cdot\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta,\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A)\big)

(J.42)

where
\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta,\bm{j}}
is the probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}_{+}\times(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})^{k^{*}}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}-1}_{+}
induced by a sequence of random elements

(\eta R_{1},\cdots,\eta R_{k^{*}},\Theta_{1},\cdots,\Theta_{k^{*}},\eta T^{\uparrow}_{2}(\eta),\cdots,\eta T^{\uparrow}_{k^{*}}(\eta))
such that

•

For any
i=1,\cdots,k^{*}
, we have
R_{i}=\textbf{T}_{r}(W^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta)),\Theta_{i}=\textbf{T}_{\theta}(W^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta))
where the distribution of
W^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta)
follows the law
\mathbb{P}\Big(V_{0}\in\cdot\ \Big|\ \eta\left\lVert V_{0}\right\rVert>\delta,\ V_{0}\in E_{\bm{j}_{i}}\Big)
; Besides,
(W^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta))_{i=1}^{k^{*}}
is an independent sequence;

•

For any
i=2,\cdots,l^{*}
, the distribution of
T^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta)
follows from
\mathbb{P}\Big(\eta T^{\delta}_{1}(\eta)\in\cdot\ \Big|\ \eta T^{\delta}_{1}(\eta)\leq 2\bar{t}\Big)
(for the definition of stopping times
T^{\delta}_{k}(\eta)
, see
eq.

J.26
); Besides,
(T^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta))_{i=1}^{k^{*}}
is an independent sequence, and it is independent of
(W^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta))_{i=1}^{k^{*}}
;

•

\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta,\bm{j}}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}\Big((\eta R_{1},\cdots,\eta R_{k^{*}},\Theta_{1},\cdots,\Theta_{k^{*}},\eta T^{\uparrow}_{2}(\eta),\cdots,\eta T^{\uparrow}_{k^{*}}(\eta))\in\cdot\Big)
.

As for weak convergence of
(\eta R_{1},\cdots,\eta R_{k^{*}},\Theta_{1},\cdots,\Theta_{k^{*}},\eta T^{\uparrow}_{2}(\eta),\cdots,\eta T^{\uparrow}_{k^{*}}(\eta))
, observe that

•

For any
i\in[k^{*}]
, from Assumption
I.6
one can see the regularly varying nature of distribution of
V_{0}
on the cone
E_{\bm{j}_{i}}
(hence for
W^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta)
as well),
thus yielding that
(\eta R_{i},\Theta_{i})\Rightarrow(R^{*}_{i},\Theta^{*}_{i})
as
\eta\downarrow 0

where
R^{*}_{i}
and
\Theta^{*}_{i}
are independent,
the law of
\Theta^{*}_{i}
is
S_{\bm{j}_{i}}
,
and the law of
R^{*}_{i}
is the Pareto distribution with

\mathbb{P}(R^{*}_{i}>x)=\frac{\delta^{\alpha_{\bm{j}_{i}}}}{(x\vee\delta)^{\alpha_{\bm{j}_{i}}}};

•

For any
x\in[0,2\bar{t}]
, since
\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor H(\delta/\eta)=0
, it is easy to show that

\lim_{\eta\downarrow 0}\frac{1-(1-H(\delta/\eta))^{\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor}}{\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor H(\delta/\eta)}=1;

therefore, we have (for any
x\in(0,2\bar{t}]
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\eta T_{1}^{\delta}(\eta)\leq x\ |\ \eta T_{1}^{\delta}(\eta)\leq 2\bar{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-(1-H(\delta/\eta))^{\lfloor x/\eta\rfloor}}{1-(1-H(\delta/\eta))^{\lfloor 2\bar{t}/\eta\rfloor}}\rightarrow\frac{x}{2\bar{t}}

as
\eta\downarrow 0
, which implies that
T^{\uparrow}_{i}(\eta)
converges weakly to a uniform RV on
[0,2\bar{t}]
.

Together with the assumption on set
A
in
eq.

J.41
,
we can now see that, if we denote the weak limit of measure
\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta,\bm{j}}
as
\mu_{\delta,\bm{j}}
,
then (the measure
\mu_{\bm{j}}
is defined in
eq.

J.40
)

\displaystyle\mu_{\delta,\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A)\big)=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\delta^{\alpha_{\bm{j}_{i}}}}{(2\bar{t})^{k^{*}-1}}\mu_{\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A)\big).

From the continuity of mapping
h
, one can see that
h^{-1}(\bar{A})
is a closed set and
h^{-1}(A^{\circ})
is an open set. Using Portmanteau theorem, we now have

\displaystyle\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\delta^{\alpha_{\bm{j}_{i}}}}{(2\bar{t})^{k^{*}-1}}\mu_{\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A^{\circ})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\eta}\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta,\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A)\big)\leq\limsup_{\eta}\mathbb{Q}_{\eta,\delta,\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A)\big)

(J.43)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\delta^{\alpha_{\bm{j}_{i}}}}{(2\bar{t})^{k^{*}-1}}\mu_{\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(\bar{A})\big).

(J.44)

Moving on, we analyze the limit of the other terms in
eq.

J.42
.
For any
i\in[k^{*}]
, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(W_{i}\in E_{i})=\frac{H_{\bm{j}_{i}}(\delta/\eta)}{H(\delta/\eta)}.

Now due to the regularly varying nature of
H_{j}
and
H
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\mathbb{P}(W_{i}\in E_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}H_{\bm{j}_{i}}(1/\eta)/H(1/\eta)}=\frac{\delta^{k^{*}\alpha_{1}}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}\delta^{\alpha_{\bm{j}_{i}}}}.

(J.45)

On the other hand, from Lemma
G.4
and the regularly varying nature of function
H
, for any fixed
\kappa>1
, we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{2}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{k^{*}-1}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa^{k^{*}-1}\limsup_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{2\bar{t}H(\delta/\eta)/\eta}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{k^{*}-1}=\kappa^{k^{*}-1},

\displaystyle\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{2}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{k^{*}-1}

\displaystyle\geq(1/\kappa)^{k^{*}-1}\liminf_{\eta\downarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}}{2\bar{t}}\cdot\frac{2\bar{t}H(\delta/\eta)/\eta}{H(1/\eta)/\eta}\Big)^{k^{*}-1}

\displaystyle=(1/\kappa)^{k^{*}-1}.

Due to arbitrariness of
\kappa>1
, we yield that

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta}\frac{\big(\mathbb{P}(T^{\prime}_{1}\leq 2\bar{t}/\eta)\big)^{k^{*}-1}}{\Big(\frac{H(1/\eta)}{\eta}\cdot\frac{2\bar{t}}{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}}\Big)^{k^{*}-1}}=1.

(J.46)

Collecting all the limits in
eq.

J.44
-
eq.

J.46
and plugging them into
eq.

J.42
, we now have established that

\displaystyle\mu_{\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(A^{\circ})\big)\leq\liminf_{\eta}\frac{p(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})}{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}H_{\bm{j}_{i}}(1/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)\eta^{k^{*}-1}}}\leq\limsup_{\eta}\frac{p(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})}{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}H_{\bm{j}_{i}}(1/\eta)}{H(1/\eta)\eta^{k^{*}-1}}}\leq\mu_{\bm{j}}\big(h^{-1}(\bar{A})\big).

To conclude the proof, recall that for any type
\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})
, we have
\#\{k=1,2,\cdots,k^{*}:\ \bm{j}_{k}=j\}=i^{*}_{j}
(i.e. for any
j\in[m]
, the number of elements in
(\bm{j}_{1},\cdots,\bm{j}_{k^{*}})
that are equal to
j
is exactly
i^{*}_{j}
), thus
\prod_{i=1}^{k^{*}}H_{\bm{j}_{i}}(1/\eta)=\prod_{k=1}^{m}\big(H_{k}(1/\eta)\big)^{i^{*}_{k}}
.
∎

More generally, for

\displaystyle p(A,\delta,\eta)=\mathbb{P}\Big(V_{0}\ \text{is of type-$(A,\delta,\eta)$}\ \Big|\ \eta\left\lVert V_{0}\right\rVert>\delta\Big),

(J.47)

we know that
p(A,\delta,\eta)=\sum_{\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})}p(A,\delta,\eta,\bm{j})
.
Also, define measure
\mu
as

\displaystyle\mu=\sum_{\bm{j}\in\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})}\mu_{\bm{j}}.

(J.48)

The next result follows immediately from Lemma
J.13
and the fact that
|\bm{j}(\bm{i}^{*})|<\infty
. Recall that
J_{\mathcal{G}}
is defined in
eq.

I.9
.

Corollary J.14
.

There exists a function
\widetilde{\lambda}(\eta)\in RV_{1+J_{\mathcal{G}}-\alpha_{1}}(\eta)
such that, given any
\delta>0
and any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\displaystyle h(r_{1},\cdots,r_{k^{*}},\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{k^{*}},t_{2},\cdots,t_{k^{*}})\in A\ \Longrightarrow\ t_{i}<2\bar{t}\ \forall i=2,\cdots,k^{*}\text{ and }r_{i}>\delta\ \forall i\in[k^{*}],

(J.49)

it holds that

\displaystyle\mu\big(h^{-1}(A^{\circ})\big)\leq\liminf_{\eta}\frac{p(A,\delta,\eta)}{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\widetilde{\lambda}(\eta)}\leq\limsup_{\eta}\frac{p(A,\delta,\eta)}{\delta^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot\widetilde{\lambda}(\eta)}\leq\mu\big(h^{-1}(\bar{A})\big).

In particular, the regularly varying function
\widetilde{\lambda}(\cdot)
admits the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}(\eta)=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{m}\big(H_{j}(1/\eta)\big)^{i^{*}_{j}}}{H(1/\eta)\eta^{k^{*}-1}}

(J.50)

Consider the following stopping times

\displaystyle\sigma(\eta)

\displaystyle=\min\{n\geq 0:X^{\eta}_{n}\notin\mathcal{G}\};

\displaystyle R(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)

\displaystyle=\min\{n\geq T^{\eta}_{1}(\delta):\left\lVert X^{\eta}_{n}\right\rVert\leq 3\epsilon\}.

Here
\sigma
indicate the first time that the iterates exit domain
\mathcal{G}
,
while
R
denotes the time the SGD iterates return to a small neighborhood of the attractor
\bm{0}

after the first large jump.
In the next few results, we study the probability of the different scenarios regarding the first exit time
\sigma(\eta)
and first return time
R(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)
.
To this end, let

\displaystyle K^{\eta}(\delta)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\max\{k\geq 0:\ T^{\eta}_{k}(\delta)\leq\sigma(\eta)\wedge R(\epsilon,\delta,\eta)\}

be the count of large jumps before the first exit or first return.
Furthermore, we introduce the following concept as the
accumulated cost
of large jumps before the first exit or return.
Let
\mathcal{J}^{\eta,\delta}_{0}=0
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\eta,\delta}_{k}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathcal{J}^{\eta,\delta}_{k-1}+J(W^{\eta}_{k}(\delta))\ \ \ \forall k\leq K^{\eta}(\delta)

where the cost function
J(\cdot)
is defined in
eq.

I.6
.
In other words,
\mathcal{J}^{\eta,\delta}_{k}
indicates the total cost of the first
k
large jumps, if there are at least
k
large jumps, before the first exit or first return.
Similarly, we can also define a step-wise accumulated cost as

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\downarrow}_{\eta,\delta}(n)=\max\{\mathcal{J}^{\eta,\delta}_{k}:\ T^{\eta}_{k}(\delta)\leq n\}\ \forall n\leq\sigma(\eta)\wedge R(\epsilon,\delta,\eta).

In other words,
\mathcal{J}^{\downarrow}_{\eta,\delta}(n)
evaluates the total cost of large jumps up until step
n
.
As a preparation for our analyses below, we first discuss the following technical tools.
For any
\epsilon>0
, let

\hat{t}(\epsilon)=c_{1}+c_{1}\log(1/\epsilon)
where
c_{1}
is the constant in Lemma
J.4
.
Besides, define
